There are no PUBLIC table/function grants. Protect the function owner, direct database writes and audit records. The operator name is user-supplied; the recorded DB session is the authenticated identity. Set `SWALLOW_ALERT_EMAIL_POLICY_CANCELLED_ENABLED=true` and/or `SWALLOW_ALERT_SMS_POLICY_CANCELLED_ENABLED=true` on `send-delivery-alert` to include each count independently in configured Slack/email alerts; both default to false. The alert login requires the count function and `alert_view` in addition to existing destination permissions. A failed check is an error and requires independent scheduler/database monitoring. Count observations and paginated lists are point-in-time, direct privileged edits can bypass the revision, and pre-migration repeated transitions cannot be reconstructed. Index creation and updates add database cost; production scale and actual external alert receipt are unverified. Docker PostgreSQL tests cover real guarded cancellations, scoped access, revision recurrence, stale acknowledgements, concurrent cancellation and permission revocation, migration upgrade and a blocked alert destination.

`SWALLOW_SMS_WORKFLOW_STEP_POLICY=snapshot|active|unchanged` selects the Twilio workflow worker policy. Default `snapshot` retains the queued template; `active` cancels when the same workflow or SMS step is unavailable or disabled; `unchanged` additionally requires the queued content to match the current content. Guarded jobs with missing workflow internal identity are cancelled, while jobs without workflow context bypass this check. The workflow row is share-locked until dispatch reservation commits and the lease is rechecked; a change after reservation cannot stop the outgoing request. Restoring the same text and provider overrides are not detected. Prior attempts remain; cancelled work is not automatically replayed; optional cancellation monitoring is described above.

Apply worker migration `0121` and grant the Twilio DB login SELECT, INSERT and UPDATE on `public.novu_rust_sms_workflow_worker_config`. Workers in one environment must register the same policy or reject startup; missing schema or privileges prevent processing and cause reconnects. Older binaries bypass registration and must be drained before relying on the guard. For a policy change, stop all affected Twilio workers, have a trusted DBA set only that environment's `step_policy` to NULL, then restart all with the same setting. Protect direct access to the registration table. Guarded dispatch adds a workflow read, row lock and lease checks. Docker PostgreSQL tests cover changed and disabled steps, concurrent edits, missing identity, policy mismatch and reconnect. Ten selected SMS scenarios match the pinned original with a mock Twilio endpoint, including one unchanged-policy send. Operational load, real SMS provider delivery and full Novu compatibility remain unverified.

`SWALLOW_EMAIL_WORKFLOW_STEP_POLICY=snapshot|active|unchanged` applies to both SendGrid and Resend workflow workers. The default `snapshot` preserves the queued template. `active` requires the same workflow identity to exist with the workflow and email step active and both templates present before dispatch reservation. `unchanged` additionally requires the queued subject and HTML templates to match the current step. Disabled, missing, or changed steps cancel the job without a provider attempt; jobs lacking a workflow internal ID are cancelled in the selected guarded modes. Manually created emails without workflow context bypass the check. The check holds a workflow row share lock through the reservation transaction and rechecks the job lease after waiting and before dispatch; a change committed before the lock is observed, while a request already reserved can still be sent. Changing text and restoring it to the same value is not detected. An override can still alter the rendered output. Cancellations are not automatically replayed; optional cancellation monitoring is described above. Review before reissuing. Existing delivery history from previous attempts is preserved.

Apply worker migration `0120` before deploying the new SendGrid or Resend binaries and grant each worker DB login SELECT, INSERT and UPDATE on `public.novu_rust_email_workflow_worker_config`. All new workers in the same environment register the same policy or reject startup; missing migration or privileges makes them reconnect without processing. The table has no default public grants. Older workers do not register or enforce the policy and must be drained before relying on it. To change policy, stop **all** affected email workers, have a trusted administrator set only that environment's `step_policy` to NULL, then restart them all with the same selected policy. Protect the registration table and restrict direct edits. Guarded jobs add a workflow read/row lock and lease checks; contention and production throughput are unverified. Docker PostgreSQL tests cover API changes, both provider queues, worker-policy mismatch, and a concurrent update; the matching SendGrid request is also compared with the original using a mock. Real delivery, automatic alerting for these cancellations and full Novu compatibility are unverified.

Worker migration `0118` and `SWALLOW_FCM_TOKEN_CLEANUP_GUARD=reference|version` choose how an invalid-token response interacts with concurrent credential changes. Default `reference` preserves the existing cleanup behavior. With `version`, a worker with cleanup enabled reads and share-locks the current matching device row immediately before dispatch, then removes the rejected token only if the row still has the same generation after the request. A device-row update or delete/reinsert changes the generation; missing rows and missing snapshots skip cleanup. Other tokens in that row may cause the whole cleanup to be skipped after an unrelated change. To make even identical FCM API registrations advance the generation, enable `SWALLOW_FCM_REASSERT_REGISTRATION=true` on all API instances; see the API README. The guard does not suppress the outgoing request, retry cleanup, protect changes before the snapshot, or detect no-op registrations accepted by old or unconfigured API instances. Directly disabling triggers or privileged writes that bypass them also defeat the guard. The generation is a random per-row revision, not an identity or secret. It adds a row read/lock per eligible dispatch and a UUID update on every device-row update even when the guard is disabled.

Apply worker migration `0118` first and replace all FCM workers; API re-registration protection also requires `0119` and its EXECUTE grant as described in the API README. Set the same guard and cleanup flags on all workers; older or unguarded workers can still remove tokens with the prior behavior. The cooperative routing guard fingerprints the non-default `version` selection; changing a guarded environment requires stopping all participating workers and using the audited routing reset described below. The default fingerprint is unchanged. Migration adds a populated UUID column and trigger to existing rows; review locking and write cost for large databases. Socket/API clients may need to refresh registration after an invalid response that is deliberately retained. Docker PostgreSQL and mock FCM tests cover unchanged rows, row updates, deletion/reinsertion, environment isolation and routing-policy mismatch. Reference comparison and production limitations are recorded in the status document.

FCM workers accept `SWALLOW_FCM_INVALID_TOKEN_POLICY=reference|unregistered` (default `reference`; invalid values fail startup). It controls the provider error classification used by optional `IS_EXPIRED_TOKENS_REMOVAL_ENABLED=true`. `reference` preserves the pinned Novu/Firebase SDK's message-based behavior, including possible false positives. `unregistered` classifies a token for cleanup only for HTTP 404 with a structured `google.firebase.fcm.v1.FcmError` detail whose `errorCode` is `UNREGISTERED`; malformed, oversized, ambiguous or different responses do not qualify. Google describes this error in its [FCM error codes](https://firebase.google.com/docs/cloud-messaging/error-codes). Other FCM errors still produce the existing rejected or unknown attempt states. This option does not enable cleanup by itself or change routed-target exclusions. The separate optional generation guard above covers some concurrent changes. The strict classifier can leave some invalid tokens in place.

Set the same cleanup flag and policy on all workers for an environment. The optional routing guard includes the non-default `unregistered` policy in its fingerprint; to change a guarded environment, stop all participating workers, use the existing audited routing reset procedure, then start all with the same policy. Unprotected and older workers ignore this protection; drain/upgrade them first. `reference` retains the previous fingerprint. This choice changes behavior from Novu and is not proof of full parity. Docker PostgreSQL and mock FCM HTTP validate stored-token cleanup, raw error false-positive avoidance, structured 404 cleanup, 500 preservation, tenant isolation and worker policy mismatch; the default classifier was compared with the pinned original SDK. Actual FCM responses and concurrent token refresh have not been tested.

Worker migration `0117` provides optional audited scope administration. A trusted DBA registers each scope administrator's environment, LOGIN name and current role OID in `public.novu_rust_operator_scope_admins`; grant that login EXECUTE on `public.novu_rust_set_operator_admin_scope(text,text,text[],text[],text)` and, if audit access is needed, `public.novu_rust_list_operator_scope_audit(text,bigint)`. No default grants are provided. A scope administrator can change any current registered grant administrator in its environment, including itself if also registered; setting NULL permits unrestricted delegation. Scope administration is therefore a privileged role, distinct from a grant administrator, and must be assigned carefully.

Use `delivery-reconcile operator-scope-set ENV DB_LOGIN '["slack_view"]' '["slack_ack"]' 'change ticket'`; each JSON argument must be an array of permission strings or `null` (unrestricted), while `[]` denies all. Changes lock the scope-administrator registration and target administrator row, verify their current LOGIN OIDs, and update the scope and audit atomically. No-op and rejected requests are not recorded. `operator-scope-audit ENV [AFTER_ID]` lists up to 100 changes as JSON, including previous/new scopes and reason; IDs are strings. The listing is a point-in-time page and ID allocation is not commit ordering, so rescan or use independent audit export. Removing scope-admin registration waits for in-flight calls; it does not undo earlier changes or revoke previously granted permissions. Apply migrations in order, restrict direct table privileges and protect the trusted function owner, schema, audit table and output. A DBA or other privileged direct SQL user can bypass this audit, and it is not tamper-proof or SSO. Docker PostgreSQL tests use real migrations, restricted logins and CLI, validate rejected requests, audit failure rollback and concurrent revocation; production deployment and load are unverified.

Worker migration `0116` optionally limits each environment-bound grant administrator's delegation. In `novu_rust_operator_grant_admins`, `grant_scope` and `revoke_scope` are independent arrays of the existing permission names. NULL means unrestricted (the migration default preserves existing administrators), and an empty array denies that action entirely. For example, a trusted DBA can configure `UPDATE public.novu_rust_operator_grant_admins SET grant_scope=ARRAY['slack_view'], revoke_scope=ARRAY['slack_view','slack_ack'] WHERE environment_id='ENV' AND database_principal='ADMIN';`. These are examples, not automatic grants. The administrator's current LOGIN OID and environment are still checked. The management function holds a share lock on the same administrator row, so a committed scope change fences later delegation; an in-flight transaction can finish before the change. Invalid scope values are rejected. Existing grants are not revoked by reducing the administrator's scope.

Grant EXECUTE on `public.novu_rust_current_operator_admin_scope(text)` to a registered administrator to let `delivery-reconcile operator-scope ENV` display its own scope as JSON; null means unrestricted. It cannot inspect another administrator or change scope. Direct DBA changes are not recorded in the delegation audit; the optional audited scope-management path is described above. Protect the functions, ownership, schema and direct grant-table access. New permissions require a migration updating both the validation constraint and delegation function. Apply 0116 after earlier migrations; replaying an older direct SQL migration that replaces the management function can remove this enforcement. Replace and restrict old privileged tools and do not give delegated managers direct grant writes. Shared DB logins do not identify individual people, and this is not SSO or immutable auditing. Docker PostgreSQL tests cover full migration order, restricted roles, independent grant/revoke scopes, audit rollback, CLI and concurrent scope reduction. Production migration, DB restoration and load remain unverified.

Slack endpoint selection is an operator choice. The default `NOVU_SLACK_WORKFLOW_ENDPOINT_POLICY=current` selects matching endpoints when fanout runs. The optional `snapshot` policy allows only endpoint records captured when the in-app worker processed the trigger and still identical at fanout; set `NOVU_SLACK_SNAPSHOT_ENDPOINTS=true` on **every** in-app worker first. New, deleted, recreated or modified endpoints cause the parent fanout to fail for review; unchanged captured endpoints may already have delivery jobs, so this can be a partial fanout. A missing snapshot fails closed, including old queued jobs or jobs produced by an unconfigured worker. No automatic replay is performed. Existing Slack pre-dispatch failure checks (`check-slack`, `SWALLOW_ALERT_SLACK_JOBS_ENABLED`, and `slack-ack`) can surface and acknowledge these failures; acknowledgement does not send or retry them.

Apply worker migration `0115` before deploying the new fanout workers. Upgrade all fanout consumers first while retaining `current`: older binaries reject the added snapshot field. Upgrade and enable snapshots on all in-app producers, then drain or review jobs produced without snapshots. Stop every affected fanout worker, have a trusted DBA set only `endpoint_policy` to NULL for that environment in `novu_rust_slack_workflow_worker_config`, and restart them all with `snapshot`. New workers reject inconsistent policies in one environment; older workers do not enforce the registration. Protect this table, and reverse the process deliberately when changing policy. The existing `NOVU_SLACK_WORKFLOW_STEP_POLICY` is independent.

Snapshot capture holds the subscriber lock and shares endpoint row locks, and stores endpoint metadata in the queued parent; protect queue access. It permits at most 1,000 endpoints per trigger and rejects larger sets. Fanout rechecks its lease and does not copy the list into each child. The guarantee starts at trigger **processing**, not HTTP acceptance; earlier endpoint changes can be included. Direct SQL writers that ignore the locking protocol, target reassignment outside Swallow, changes after dispatch reservation, old binaries, and actual Slack delivery are outside this guarantee. Extra reads, locks and queue storage have not been load tested. This is a Swallow option, not a claim of complete Novu parity.

The bounded original-server comparison below was also run with `NOVU_SLACK_SNAPSHOT_ENDPOINTS=true NOVU_SLACK_WORKFLOW_ENDPOINT_POLICY=snapshot`; two mocked Slack request bodies matched. It does not test live Slack or the complete original contract.

**Current deployment guidance:** apply API and worker migrations in order through the latest version before deploying matching binaries. Read [the current implementation status](../../../RUST_IMPLEMENTATION_STATUS_KO.md) for migration prerequisites, role identity, grants, options and known limitations. This is not yet full Novu compatibility or verified production HA. Do not blindly replay older permission migrations: once newer permissions are granted, old constraints can fail and old functions can weaken authorization. Use `migrate-worker` to track each service migration history as described below. Review existing schemas before changing them.

## Service migration history

Use a trusted migration DB login and `DATABASE_URL`. On a new shared database, apply `migrate-worker apply api ../../api/rust-api/migrations --through 17`, then `migrate-worker apply worker migrations`, `migrate-worker apply api ../../api/rust-api/migrations`, and `migrate-worker apply ws ../../ws/rust-ws/migrations` (paths are relative to this directory). API migration 0018 depends on a worker table, so applying the complete API set first fails. The legacy `migrate-worker apply migrations` remains an alias for worker. The runner validates contiguous files and SHA-256 history, serializes concurrent runs with a PostgreSQL advisory transaction lock, and applies all pending SQL and history rows for the selected service atomically. API, worker and WS have separate history tables and share the same database advisory lock. Re-running skips recorded files; altered or missing historical files are rejected. Migration 0023's own transaction wrapper is incorporated into the runner transaction. Other standalone transaction-control lines are rejected. Protect the migration directory, history table, public schema and function owners; manual SQL bypasses this history. Applying all pending migrations together can hold locks for a long time. Cross-service atomicity, automatic dependency validation, recovery after uncertain commit acknowledgement, production permissions and scale are not handled or verified.

An existing untracked service anchor table is not automatically migrated. Only after independently verifying the existing schema and exact applied worker version may a trusted administrator run `migrate-worker baseline SERVICE MIGRATION_DIR VERSION I-VERIFIED-THE-EXISTING-SCHEMA`, then `apply`. Baseline records local checksums without inspecting or changing the schema: an incorrect version or mismatched files can hide missing migrations. Anchor-table detection is not a comprehensive discovery of every possible partial schema. A disposable PostgreSQL test covers real API, worker and WS migrations in dependency order, reruns, checksum mismatch, rollback, concurrent runners and explicit baseline; production upgrade and restoration are untested.

The bounded original-server Slack comparison now creates the Rust integration, connection and step through experimental management APIs and exercises `unchanged` when processing the queued fanout and deliveries. For the two channel/user endpoints, mocked Slack request bodies match the pinned original server's HTTP trigger and worker output. The original configuration is seeded directly in MongoDB; Rust fanout/delivery run through a test harness rather than the production worker binary. This does not compare the management API contract, real Slack, or every workflow case. Reproduce with `NOVU_REFERENCE_SOURCE=/absolute/path NOVU_TEST_PREFERENCE_DELIVERY=1 NOVU_TEST_SNOOZE_ONLY=1 NOVU_TEST_SLACK_ONLY=1 NOVU_TEST_MONGO_REPLICA=1 ./scripts/test-reference-subscribers.sh` from `apps/api/rust-api`.

`slack-workflow-worker` supports `NOVU_SLACK_WORKFLOW_STEP_POLICY=snapshot|active|unchanged`. The default `snapshot` preserves queued template behavior. `active` rechecks the current workflow and chat step before fanout and before reserving each delivery; `unchanged` also requires the current content to equal the queued template. Disabled/removed steps or changed content (when `unchanged`) cancel the affected jobs; they are not automatically replayed if reenabled. A change and later restoration of identical text is not detected. The selected modes lock the workflow row until fanout or dispatch reservation commits and check the job lease again after waiting; changes committed before the lock are observed. An external request may still start or finish after the reservation commits, even if the step is disabled immediately afterward. Apply worker migration `0113` before deploying new workers. The policy is registered per environment alongside the token-map fingerprint; different new-worker policies or token maps are rejected. Stop old workers before relying on this because older binaries ignore the policy column. To change policy, stop all affected workers, have a trusted administrator set the environment row's `step_policy` to NULL, then restart all with the same selected policy; do not clear the credential fingerprint unless also rotating credentials. Protect the registration table and DB permissions. Each guarded fanout adds a workflow row read/lock and lease check; each delivery adds those plus another lease check before reservation. Contention and throughput under production load are unverified. Docker PostgreSQL tests cover changed content, disabled steps, old snapshot behavior, competing updates and worker config mismatch, plus an API-to-queued-delivery test; actual Slack delivery during concurrent policy changes and full Novu parity are unverified.

Worker migration `0114` adds review for jobs cancelled by the `active`/`unchanged` Slack step policy. `delivery-reconcile list-slack ENV` includes unacknowledged `stepPolicyCancelled` jobs, and an authorized `slack_ack` operator can use `slack-ack ENV JOB OPERATOR REASON` to acknowledge them without changing or replaying jobs. To include the unacknowledged count and return exit 2 in `check-slack`, set `SWALLOW_SLACK_CHECK_POLICY_CANCELLED=true`; the default is false. To include it in `send-delivery-alert`, independently set `SWALLOW_ALERT_SLACK_POLICY_CANCELLED_ENABLED=true`; it can be used without the other Slack checks. With scoped access, grant the relevant DB login EXECUTE on `public.novu_rust_slack_policy_cancelled(text)` and the environment's `slack_view`; listing and acknowledgement retain their existing separate function and permissions. Direct mode instead requires SELECT on the environment, queue, Slack attempt and Slack acknowledgement tables and does not itself enforce environment-specific access. New-condition cooldown treats this as a separate problem category. Acknowledgement records the current job revision: a later cancellation is reported again. Both operations and CLI tools require migration `0114`; deploy matching binaries and all referenced DB permissions before enabling the check. The default check remains unchanged, but the updated listing includes this category regardless of the flag. This is a point-in-time count; manual DB edits or old binaries can bypass it, cancellations with a preexisting attempt are excluded, and an acknowledgement does not prove delivery. The index adds writes and can block during migration. Docker PostgreSQL tests exercise a real guarded cancellation, scoped/direct counts, environment isolation, CLI, blocked mock alert, acknowledgement, recurrence and permission revocation. Actual external alerts and production scale are unverified.

`send-delivery-alert ENV GRACE_SECONDS HEARTBEAT_MAX_SECONDS COOLDOWN_SECONDS` sends enabled unhealthy environment checks to configured Slack and/or Resend email destinations. Apply worker migrations in order through `0094` and the relevant API migrations; see the current status document for dependencies. The sender DB login also needs an explicit `alert_send` row in `novu_rust_delivery_operator_grants` for that environment. Configure Slack with `SWALLOW_ALERT_SLACK_WEBHOOK` or `SWALLOW_ALERT_SLACK_WEBHOOK_FILE`; email uses `SWALLOW_ALERT_EMAIL_TO`, `NOVU_RESEND_FROM`, and `NOVU_RESEND_API_KEY` or `NOVU_RESEND_API_KEY_FILE`. Both destinations may be used together. Give each destination a stable, non-secret `SWALLOW_ALERT_SLACK_TARGET_ID` or `SWALLOW_ALERT_EMAIL_TARGET_ID` (default `default`); use a new ID if the destination changes. Environment and target IDs have restricted ASCII syntax. Only the environment and aggregate status of enabled checks are sent. Slack URLs must use the official Slack or Slack Gov HTTPS webhook hosts, and redirects are disabled. Credentials are not stored in PostgreSQL.

Set `SWALLOW_ALERT_EMAIL_PROVIDER=sendgrid` to use SendGrid for either alert command instead of the default `resend`. Configure `SWALLOW_ALERT_EMAIL_TO`, `NOVU_SENDGRID_FROM`, `NOVU_SENDGRID_API_KEY` or `NOVU_SENDGRID_API_KEY_FILE`, optional `NOVU_SENDGRID_SENDER_NAME`, and optional `NOVU_SENDGRID_REGION=global|eu`. An unsupported provider or region fails that destination configuration; a separately configured Slack destination is still attempted. Assign a new non-secret email target ID when changing provider or recipient, and coordinate changes across senders: the target ID identifies the shared cooldown/attempt slot, not the provider or actual address. Both providers use the existing durable reservation and manual resolution policy; SendGrid has no Resend idempotency key, and HTTP acceptance is not inbox delivery. Rejected or uncertain requests need operator review, and uncoordinated fallback can duplicate alerts. Mock HTTP tests cover SendGrid acceptance, rejection, unknown outcomes, fallback and scoped PostgreSQL reservations; actual SendGrid alert delivery, external webhook correlation and Novu parity for this operational feature are not verified.

Alert checks are selectable independently of transport. By default the existing Resend check remains enabled. Set `SWALLOW_ALERT_RESEND_ENABLED=false` to omit it, set `SWALLOW_ALERT_QUEUE_MAX_WAIT_SECONDS` to 1–86400 to include overdue queue jobs and expired leases, and set `SWALLOW_ALERT_ATTEMPTS_ENABLED=true` to include unknown or stranded SendGrid, Resend, Twilio and FCM attempts. Booleans must be exactly `true` or `false`; at least one check must be enabled. The positional grace and heartbeat arguments are still required integers but are ignored when Resend checking is disabled. Each enabled check requires its existing migrations and DB read permissions. If a check fails, the command runs the other checks and attempts an alert naming the failed checks, then exits 1 even if a destination accepts it. Invalid thresholds fail before checking or sending. Monitor execution errors independently: a DB outage can prevent both checking and delivery. All enabled signals share a destination slot. The default cooldown can delay a second incident; optionally choose `SWALLOW_ALERT_COOLDOWN_POLICY=new-condition` after migration `0085` to bypass an accepted cooldown for a newly observed problem category. This does not bypass unknown or rejected outcomes. Checks are separate snapshots, not one atomic view. They do not detect every failure or prove receipt.

Use an authorized grant administrator to provision the exact DB login and environment, for example:

```sh
delivery-reconcile operator-grant ENV scheduler-login alert_send "change ticket"
delivery-reconcile operator-grant ENV reviewer-login alert_retry "change ticket"
```

Grant `alert_accepted` separately when needed. Bootstrap the grant administrator as described in the current status document; the management function records the login's current role OID and an audit entry. A direct three-column insert leaves the OID unset on current installations and will not authorize the login. Restrict function owners, the schema and direct table privileges.

Worker migration `0112` adds environment-scoped administrative review without direct grant/audit table reads. Provision an environment-specific row in `novu_rust_operator_grant_admins` bound to the current LOGIN OID, and grant that login EXECUTE on `public.novu_rust_list_operator_grants(text,text,text)` and/or `public.novu_rust_list_operator_grant_audit(text,bigint)`. Listing EXECUTE alone does not authorize granting or revoking; do not grant the management function to a read-only reviewer. `delivery-reconcile operator-grants ENV [AFTER_LOGIN AFTER_PERMISSION]` lists at most 100 grants ordered by login and permission in C collation; `identityCurrent` checks only whether the stored OID matches a currently LOGIN-enabled role. `delivery-reconcile operator-grant-audit ENV [AFTER_ID]` lists up to 100 changes in increasing ID order, including operator-supplied reasons; IDs are JSON strings and can be passed unchanged as the next cursor. Both require the same environment-bound administrator row and lock it for the statement/transaction, so revocation waits for an in-flight read. A page is a point-in-time query, not a consistent full scan: grants may change between pages, and audit ID allocation can precede transaction commit, so rescan from the beginning or use an appropriate independent audit system. Rejected/no-op operations and direct SQL changes are not recorded. The functions do not provide tamper-proof auditing; protect owners, schema, administrator rows, output and reasons, and avoid secrets in reasons. Role OIDs can change after restore and need review. The indexes add writes and can lock or take time to build on an existing large database. Docker PostgreSQL tests verify restricted logins, both CLI commands and pagination, stale role identity, environment isolation and revocation fencing; production migration/load and external audit export are not verified.

Migrations `0090` and `0091` add optional monitoring of all unacknowledged failed queue jobs. Set `SWALLOW_ALERT_FAILED_JOBS_ENABLED=true` to include the count in alerts. `check-failed-jobs ENV` and `list-failed-jobs ENV [AFTER_JOB]` require `alert_view` and EXECUTE on `novu_rust_failed_queue_count(text)` and `novu_rust_failed_queue_list(text,uuid)`. A separately granted `queue_ack` login with EXECUTE on `novu_rust_ack_failed_queue(text,uuid,text,text)` can use `ack-failed-job ENV JOB OPERATOR REASON`. It records the current attempt/error snapshot, without changing the job or suppressing separate uncertain-delivery and Slack checks. A transition away from and back to failed, or a changed attempt count or error while failed, advances a database failure revision and returns the job to attention even if the later error is identical. An UPDATE that leaves status, attempts and error unchanged is not a new failure; identical failures before migration 0091 cannot be distinguished retroactively. Historical failures are included, overlapping checks can alert twice, and failure diagnostics are stored in the protected acknowledgement table. Do not reapply 0090 after 0091: it can replace the new acknowledgement function with an incompatible older version. Direct writes to failure revisions or changes to the trigger bypass the protection. Listings omit payload and error text, page by UUID and are not a consistent snapshot. Disposable PostgreSQL tests cover restricted logins, a directly enqueued push workflow job that the FCM worker fails before dispatch when the subscriber is absent, acknowledgement, worker reprocessing, CLI, pagination and blocked mock alert routing. A separate original/Rust server comparison triggers a real push workflow over HTTP, deletes the subscriber before processing, compares selected failure and non-delivery behavior, and then exercises the Rust failure monitoring and acknowledgement CLI. This monitoring CLI has no original counterpart in the comparison. Providers are mocked; real FCM requests, external alert receipt and all failure combinations remain untested.

## Independent database probe alert

`send-database-alert MONITOR_ENV TARGET_LABEL COOLDOWN_SECONDS` probes a target PostgreSQL connection and role in one query while storing alert reservations in a separately provisioned alert-state database. Set `DATABASE_URL` to the alert-state database and `SWALLOW_DB_PROBE_URL` to the target; place them in independent failure domains. `SWALLOW_DB_PROBE_TIMEOUT_SECONDS` defaults to 5 and accepts 1–60 seconds. Apply API/worker migrations and provision the monitor environment, `alert_send` and the selected delivery/observation function permissions in the state DB. Existing Slack and Resend settings, `SWALLOW_ALERT_SCOPED_DELIVERY`, and cooldown policy apply. Default destination IDs are `db-TARGET_LABEL`; optional `SWALLOW_DB_ALERT_SLACK_TARGET_ID` and `SWALLOW_DB_ALERT_EMAIL_TARGET_ID` override them. Use unique non-secret IDs for each target/destination. Labels accept 1–48 ASCII alphanumeric, dot, hyphen or underscore characters and are included in the alert; URLs and credentials are not.

Set `SWALLOW_DB_PROBE_ROLE=any|writable-primary|standby` to select the expected role; default `any` checks connectivity. `writable-primary` also requires `pg_is_in_recovery()` to be false and this probe session's `transaction_read_only` to be off; `standby` requires recovery to be active. A mismatch uses the existing failed-probe alert and exit 2; invalid settings exit 1. Choose separate stable labels and destination IDs per independently monitored target. The alert payload reports a failed probe, not the precise cause. Role checks run in a single query with the same connection timeout. They do not perform a write, check table permissions, prove other sessions are writable, verify replication freshness or synchronous replication, detect a split brain, fence an old primary, or guarantee future availability. Multi-host URLs can select a different host on each connection; use explicit target URLs if each node must be monitored. Docker PostgreSQL tests verify actual primary and physical standby roles, a read-only session, and the real alert command with matching and mismatching roles; real external alert delivery and production failover are unverified. This is a Swallow operational extension, not a Novu API.

Choose `SWALLOW_DB_PROBE_ROLE=synchronous-primary` to additionally require this probe session's `synchronous_commit` to be `on` or `remote_apply`, a nonempty `synchronous_standby_names`, and at least `SWALLOW_DB_PROBE_MIN_SYNC_STANDBYS` (default 1, allowed 1–32) streaming `sync` or `quorum` senders with a reported flush LSN. Senders using an active logical replication slot are excluded. Optionally set `SWALLOW_DB_PROBE_MAX_REPLAY_LAG_BYTES` to an integer from 0 through 1099511627776 (1 TiB): only senders with a non-null replay LSN between the current primary WAL position and that many bytes behind it count. Leave it unset to preserve the previous behavior. This option applies only to `synchronous-primary`; an invalid value rejects startup. The probe login must be a member of `pg_read_all_stats` (or be a superuser); lack of membership, missing visible senders or a query error is unhealthy. Granting statistics access exposes operational metadata, so use a dedicated probe login and restrict it. An invalid minimum is a configuration error. This is an instantaneous observation, not proof that each application transaction uses synchronous commit, that the configured quorum is sufficient, that any specific node has the latest WAL or can be promoted, or that two primaries are fenced. A reported replay position can be stale; a byte threshold is not elapsed-time lag and the probe does not perform a test commit. Sender classification depends on PostgreSQL's visible statistics. Docker tests cover a physical synchronous standby, paused replay with generated WAL, minimum count, permission grant/revocation, session commit mode and an actual alert command against a server with no standby. The failover script also creates a `test_decoding` logical receiver alongside the physical standby in synchronous quorum and checks, with a restricted statistics login, that only the physical standby counts. Other replication configurations, real external alert delivery and operational failover remain unverified.

For `synchronous-primary`, set `SWALLOW_DB_PROBE_REJECT_LOGICAL_SYNC=true` to report unhealthy if any visible sender in `sync` or `quorum` state owns an active logical slot. Default `false` preserves the existing physical-sender count; invalid values reject startup. The opt-in policy is conservative: even if another standby would be sufficient, a participating logical sender triggers the alert. It does not parse configured quorum membership, predict a logical sender connecting later, prove every acknowledged commit reached a physical standby, or protect against missing/inaccurate PostgreSQL statistics. It has no effect on other probe roles. A Docker primary with an actual physical standby and `test_decoding` logical receiver verifies the optional policy on and off. The real alert command also returns 0 with the policy off and 2 with it on, reaching an intentionally blocked Slack reservation; the test uses the primary as both probe and alert-state database and sends nothing externally. Invalid startup configuration was checked separately. External alert receipt and production failover remain unverified.

Exit 0 means the probe succeeded; 2 means the probe failed, including when alert delivery needs review; 1 means configuration, state DB or dispatch failed. Authentication or transient latency can produce a failed probe without proving the whole DB is down. Run under an external scheduler and monitor its execution and the state DB independently. The shared PostgreSQL TLS settings apply to both connections. The probe and initial state DB connection have timeouts. `SWALLOW_DB_ALERT_OPERATION_TIMEOUT_SECONDS` limits each destination observation and each full reservation/send/result operation (default 30, range 1–300 seconds); it is not a single deadline for the whole command. An operation can time out after the provider received the request, leaving an uncertain outcome that requires the existing lease recovery and operator procedure. Database-side queries or locks may persist briefly after a network interruption, so supervise the process and DB independently. Shared failure domains, unsampled outages, unknown provider outcomes and actual recipient delivery remain limitations. `scripts/test-database-alert.sh` stops and restarts one of three disposable PostgreSQL containers and checks limited-login, a locked state row, a blocked alert slot and state-DB failure behavior; separate local mock HTTP verifies alert content and unknown recovery after a send timeout. No real external alert was sent.

Choose `SWALLOW_DB_ALERT_STATE_FAILURE_MODE=secondary-store` and set `SWALLOW_DB_ALERT_BACKUP_DATABASE_URL` to use a separately provisioned secondary alert-state database when the primary state DB cannot be reached at initial connection. Apply API/worker migrations and create the same monitor environment and sender permissions in that database. The target probe may be healthy: the alert still reports the primary state DB failure, and the command exits 1 even if a provider accepts it. If the secondary is also unavailable, the command fails without switching to uncoordinated delivery. It never switches after the primary connects because a delivery may already be in progress. Each state DB has independent slots and cooldown history; switching can duplicate alerts or suppress a repeat during the secondary cooldown, and secondary recovery observations are not updated while primary is active. Use genuinely independent failure domains and monitor both stores. The Docker test uses three PostgreSQL containers and a blocked secondary slot, verifies failed secondary and no switching after a post-connect permission failure; actual external delivery and production failover are unverified.

For an explicit best-effort fallback when the alert-state DB cannot be reached at initial connection, set `SWALLOW_DB_ALERT_STATE_FAILURE_MODE=send-uncoordinated`; the default is `report-only`. The fallback directly sends the target probe result and state-DB failure to configured Slack and/or the selected email provider, even when the target is healthy. It cannot consult DB permissions, reserve slots, apply cooldowns or preserve audit/outcome state. Every invocation uses a new Resend idempotency key, so repeated or concurrent invocations can duplicate messages; unknown outcomes cannot be reconciled by the DB tools. It does not retry internally, attempts both destinations independently and returns 1 even if a provider accepts. A connection failure includes authentication failures. The fallback is not used for errors after the state DB connection succeeds, where a prior request may already have started. Protect the scheduler, configuration and credentials and independently monitor its exit status. Mock HTTP tests cover the two fallback transports and independent failure handling; the actual CLI fallback against external providers and multi-host outages are not tested.

For DB-coordinated sends in either alert command, `SWALLOW_ALERT_DELIVERY_POLICY=parallel` uses a second connection to the same selected alert-state database and starts the Slack and email reservations/sends concurrently; the default `sequential` retains the original order. Invalid values fail startup. Each destination keeps its own reservation, lease, timeout and result, and permission checks still apply to the same DB login on each connection. The second connection is made before either send; if it fails, both destinations are attempted sequentially and the command reports an error. `send-database-alert` connects both clients to the selected primary or secondary state DB, not different stores. This adds one DB connection and simultaneous provider requests; the command waits for both, and health checks and condition observations remain sequential. A timeout after a request starts remains an unknown outcome requiring review, and independent state DB failovers still cannot guarantee no duplicates. Docker PostgreSQL and mocked HTTP verify separate restricted DB sessions, email completion while Slack is held, blocked destinations through the actual commands, a login connection limit that forces the sequential fallback, and parallel routing through the secondary state DB. Real providers, production failover and connection failure under load are unverified.

When using `send-uncoordinated`, set `SWALLOW_DB_ALERT_FALLBACK_DELIVERY=parallel` to start Slack and the selected email provider concurrently, each with its own operation timeout. The default `sequential` waits for Slack before starting email. Invalid values fail startup, even if fallback is not selected. Parallel mode can reduce the delay to the second destination but increases simultaneous outbound traffic; the command still waits for both outcomes and retains the uncoordinated mode’s duplicate, no-audit and unknown-outcome limitations. It does not control the normal database-coordinated path, which has its own `SWALLOW_ALERT_DELIVERY_POLICY` setting. A local mock holds the Slack response and verifies email starts before it completes only in parallel mode; real providers and process termination during simultaneous sends are unverified.

Migration `0094` replaces the raw diagnostic text in queue and Slack acknowledgement unique indexes with a fixed-size SHA-256 digest. This permits long, incompressible diagnostics that exceeded PostgreSQL B-tree entry limits; the full diagnostic remains in the restricted audit row, and the digest is also sensitive derived data. The migration rebuilds indexes and may hold locks, so assess database size and schedule before applying. Do not replay 0091/0092 afterward. A digest collision is theoretically possible. Disposable PostgreSQL 17 tests reproduce the prior failure and verify upgrade, reapplication, existing records, idempotence and recurrence; production scale and other PostgreSQL versions have not been tested.

Migration `0089` adds environment-scoped aggregate functions for queue, attempt, Resend and Twilio health. Set `SWALLOW_ALERT_CHECK_SCOPED_ACCESS=true` for the alert command or `SWALLOW_MONITOR_SCOPED_ACCESS=true` for `check`, `check-queue`, `check-resend`, `check-twilio`, and provider metrics commands. Provision `alert_view` and EXECUTE only on the functions used: `novu_rust_queue_health(text,integer)`, `novu_rust_delivery_health(text)` and `novu_rust_provider_health(text,text,integer,integer)`. These checks do not require direct queue or attempt-table reads. Slack checks use the separate `slack_view` and `SWALLOW_ALERT_SLACK_SCOPED_ACCESS` settings. The default false retains direct queries. Checks are separate snapshots and not a guarantee of delivery. Scoped health, environment isolation, permissions, equivalence and CLI behavior using a restricted DB login were tested against disposable PostgreSQL; operational privileges and external alert receipt remain unverified.

With migration `0093`, the `new-condition` cooldown policy also observes recovery: after a successful check clears a condition from an accepted slot, the same condition can bypass cooldown if it recurs. Grant the sender EXECUTE on `public.novu_rust_observe_alert_conditions(text,text,text,integer,integer)` and its environment's `alert_send`. Failed or disabled checks are not considered recovered; dispatching, unknown or rejected slots are not changed. A recovery and recurrence between scheduled observations is not detected, and concurrent schedulers can observe different snapshots. An observation failure is reported with exit 1 while other eligible alert dispatches are still attempted. The default destination policy does not use this function. Disposable PostgreSQL verifies restricted-login CLI recovery, repeated incident reservation, failed/disabled checks and unknown-slot preservation; actual external alert delivery and distributed failures remain unverified.

With migration `0088`, set `SWALLOW_ALERT_SCOPED_DELIVERY=true` to reserve and finish alert attempts through the restricted `novu_rust_reserve_alert(text,text,text,integer,integer)` and `novu_rust_finish_alert(text,text,text,uuid,text,integer)` functions. Grant EXECUTE on both and the environment-specific `alert_send` permission to the sender; the delivery path then needs no direct alert-slot or attempt table privileges. Health checks still need their own access. A reservation is bound to the authenticated session login and its role OID: another login cannot finish it. Revoking `alert_send` prevents new reservations but an already-started request can finish if that login retains function EXECUTE. Revoking function access or stopping the process can instead leave the result unknown. Shared DB logins do not identify individuals and `alert_send` is not target-specific. Direct table access and old binaries bypass this boundary; protect the function owner, schema and tables. Default `false` retains the existing direct-table path. Docker PostgreSQL tests cover scoped concurrent senders, revocation, late responses and mock Slack/Resend delivery using a restricted DB login; production roles and external alert receipt remain unverified.

The command initializes and dispatches each configured destination independently: a malformed destination or dispatch error does not prevent attempting the other one. It returns exit 1 if any configuration or dispatch operation failed, even if another channel was accepted or the checks were healthy; exit 2 indicates a destination requiring review when no other error occurred. Generic errors avoid logging credentials. Inspect each destination and monitor exit 1 separately; a shared database or health-check failure can still prevent all delivery. The CLI tests exercise independent configuration and dispatch failures against blocked database slots; actual external delivery in a partial-failure case has not been tested.

PostgreSQL reservations fence concurrent schedulers. Accepted attempts have a 60-86400 second cooldown measured from dispatch start; a new incident during cooldown may be delayed. Rejected and ambiguous attempts block further sends until operator review. Expired leases become unknown when a later run checks the slot; late responses cannot overwrite recovery. A transport failure may still mean provider acceptance. Both channels are attempted independently, subject to shared database and health-check availability. Provider acceptance is not proof of user receipt. Run through an external scheduler and independently monitor command errors, missing executions, and database outages.

`delivery-reconcile check-alert-delivery ENV` reports unknown, rejected, and expired slots (exit 2; errors exit 1). `alert-status ENV` shows up to 100 non-secret targets, including the current `attemptId`. With migration `0087`, `SWALLOW_ALERT_STATUS_SCOPED_ACCESS=true`, `alert_view` and EXECUTE on `public.novu_rust_alert_status(text)` and `public.novu_rust_alert_health(text)`, status and health use environment-scoped functions instead of direct table reads. Worker migration `0131` adds `alert-resolve-v2 ENV CHANNEL TARGET ATTEMPT_ID retry|accepted OPERATOR REASON`. Grant its DB login EXECUTE on `public.novu_rust_resolve_alert_v2(text,text,text,uuid,text,text,text)` and the environment's `alert_retry` or `alert_accepted` permission. The function locks the permission and slot, checks the supplied attempt ID against the current attempt, and records the decision with the existing audit in the same transaction. A stale ID or a state already resolved is rejected; re-read status and independently investigate the new attempt. The legacy `alert-resolve` and six-argument function remain for compatibility but lack this guard: deploy the updated CLI, grant the new function, then revoke legacy function execution and direct table access from operators to enforce fencing. Function ownership and other privileged DB access must also be protected.

Accepted is limited to unknown outcomes and is an operator assertion, not delivery evidence. Before retrying, independently verify non-acceptance: a mistaken retry can duplicate an alert. A lost response has an unknown outcome; inspect the current slot and audit before trying again. `attemptId` prevents confusing a later attempt with the one reviewed; it does not attest to provider receipt or stop a privileged actor or legacy binary with legacy permissions. Shared DB logins do not identify individuals, and audit tampering remains unresolved. Docker PostgreSQL tests exercise stale IDs, concurrent decisions, separate permissions, cross-environment denial, audit-failure rollback, and both Slack and email slots with a restricted login. Actual Slack or alert-email receipt, external scheduler integration and complete HA remain unverified.

# Experimental PostgreSQL queue primitives

The ignored integration test `TEST_DATABASE_URL=postgres://... cargo test --locked --test slack_cooldown_process_ha -- --ignored` requires a fully migrated **disposable** PostgreSQL database and an administrative login. It starts two actual `slack-worker` processes with the same active shared cooldown, waits for both connections, confirms ten jobs are deferred, kills one process, confirms the survivor defers ten more, terminates its PostgreSQL connection, waits for a new connection, and confirms ten further jobs are deferred without recording a provider attempt. The connection termination occurs between jobs, not during a dispatch or database transaction. It tests one host and one database only; it does not simulate a real 429, requests already in flight, host failure, database failover, network partitions, limited production database grants or actual Slack delivery. Never use production data or credentials for this test.

The experimental `slack-worker` sends **standalone** PostgreSQL chat jobs through Slack `chat.postMessage` using `NOVU_SLACK_ENVIRONMENT_ID` and either `NOVU_SLACK_BOT_TOKEN` or `NOVU_SLACK_BOT_TOKEN_FILE`, plus `DATABASE_URL`. Apply worker migrations through `0071`. By default the token is read once; the optional file reload is described above. It uses the shared 16 KiB single-line secret rules and never goes in the queue or attempt record. Run under a supervisor. A job has kind `experimental_slack_chat` and payload `{"channel":"C123","text":"hello","blocks":[...]}`; `blocks` is optional, and `channel` accepts a Slack channel or user ID. The caller must authorize and enqueue the job in the correct environment. Payloads contain message text and target in PostgreSQL and must be protected. This is **not connected to workflow execution, subscriber credentials or Novu chat/integration APIs**, and has no subscriber deletion or preference revalidation. It supports text and native block arrays only; rich-card rendering, provider overrides, webhook targets, all validation and full original behavior remain unimplemented. Use only in an isolated evaluation environment.

The worker records `dispatching` before posting and fences state updates with a 60-second lease. Expired leases before the attempt return to pending; after an attempt they become `unknown` and are not automatically resent. A `2xx` Slack response is accepted only with `ok: true` and nonempty channel and timestamp. Slack `fatal_error`, `internal_error`, network errors, 5xx, redirects, malformed or oversized replies and incomplete success are unknown; a small list of known rejection errors and HTTP 4xx other than 408 and 409 are rejected. Provider responses and tokens are not logged. These outcomes describe API acceptance, not a person's reading. [Slack documents](https://docs.slack.dev/reference/methods/chat.postmessage) that some errors can occur after partial success. A worker or DB failure still requires external supervision; no Slack callback reconciliation exists.

`delivery-reconcile check-slack ENV` counts unknown, rejected and stranded attempts, returning 2 if any require review, 0 when clear, and 1 on errors. Set `SWALLOW_ALERT_SLACK_JOBS_ENABLED=true` to include those counts in configured operational Slack/email alerts. To make an explicit, audited operator decision, provision `slack_retry` or `slack_accepted` in `novu_rust_delivery_operator_grants` for the exact environment and DB login, grant that login EXECUTE on `public.novu_rust_resolve_slack(text,uuid,text,text,text)` from migration `0083`, and run `delivery-reconcile slack-resolve ENV JOB retry|accepted OPERATOR REASON`. These permissions are distinct from existing notification or alert decisions; `accepted` is allowed only for an unknown attempt and is an operator assertion without a provider ID. Retry permits unknown or rejected outcomes; independently confirm non-acceptance first, because an incorrect retry can duplicate a message. The audit is not tamper-proof and DB access must prevent direct modifications. Apply `0071` after `0067` and `0068`; do not reapply older grant-constraint migrations after issuing Slack grants. Use `list-slack ENV [AFTER_JOB]` with `slack_view` and the 0081 list function to identify affected jobs without payloads; this is not provider evidence.

Worker migration `0092` preserves existing Slack workflow acknowledgements but adds a revision so the same pre-dispatch failure or missing-endpoint result is counted again after a state transition. Changes to attempts or error also advance the revision; a no-op update does not. Similar recurrences before migration cannot be distinguished retroactively. Apply `0092` before deploying the updated tools, replace old monitoring binaries, and do not reapply `0081`, `0082` or `0084` afterward because they overwrite the revised functions. Protect the revision trigger and tables from direct writes. Disposable PostgreSQL tests cover upgrade and reapplication, recurrence in both categories and existing Slack workflow/CLI behavior; production upgrade and all concurrent failures remain unverified.

Docker PostgreSQL and loopback HTTP tests cover simultaneous claims, pre/post-dispatch expiry, late-response fencing, tenant scope, secret file configuration, monitoring and audited decisions. A pinned Novu provider comparison covers two channel/user core payloads with mocked HTTP. Real Slack delivery, full original errors, channel workflow and management APIs, arbitrary provider configuration, load and full HA are not tested or implemented.

`twilio-projection-worker` optionally projects Twilio SMS callbacks to the separate `twilio_delivery_result` on queue jobs and SMS history. Apply worker migrations through `0069` and API Twilio webhook migrations through `0007`, configure the attempt-specific signed callback URL, `DATABASE_URL` and `NOVU_TWILIO_ENVIRONMENT_ID`, and run under a supervisor. A current accepted attempt with a known provider SID and matching attempt-specific callback is required. Results are `delivered`, `failed`, `pending`, `conflicting`, or `unknown`; missing, legacy, unrecognized, or ambiguous evidence is unknown. With a current dispatch-bound callback, contradictory terminal statuses, different SIDs, a duplicate provider SID within the environment, or multiple accounts conflict. Callbacks can be delayed or lost, so later scans may change results; the value is a provider report, not proof of reading. The projection leaves queue status, message status and dispatch state unchanged and does not retry or automatically resolve an unknown attempt. A retry via the updated reconciliation tool clears both result columns in the same transaction. Apply `0069`, then replace all old reconciliation binaries before starting the projector: old retry code does not clear the result. `sms-delivery` now also returns `providerIdKnown` and flags a mismatched SID or multiple accounts as conflict.

`delivery-reconcile check-twilio ENV GRACE_SECONDS HEARTBEAT_MAX_SECONDS` exits 2 for old non-cancelled SMS jobs whose result is not `delivered`, or an absent/stale projector heartbeat; healthy is 0 and errors are 1. Thresholds are 1–86400 seconds. `check-twilio-metrics` returns the same counts as Prometheus gauges and exits 0 after a successful query even when unhealthy. To include this check in Slack and/or email alerts, set both `SWALLOW_ALERT_TWILIO_GRACE_SECONDS` and `SWALLOW_ALERT_TWILIO_HEARTBEAT_MAX_SECONDS`; omit both to disable it. These thresholds are independent of the positional Resend thresholds and may be combined with other checks. One missing or invalid threshold fails configuration. Failed checks are named in attempted alerts and still produce exit 1. Schedule the commands externally and independently monitor DB errors, missing executions and destination delivery.

Age is measured from job `available_at`, including jobs that have not yet been dispatched; future and cancelled jobs are excluded. Old failures remain counted until resolved or removed, and an environment without SMS traffic still needs a projector heartbeat when this check is enabled. A heartbeat can be fresh while locked jobs were skipped. A provider's delivered report is not proof of reading; lost or unlinked callbacks may cause prolonged alarms. Current checks scan historical jobs and have not been load-tested. Docker PostgreSQL tests the real CLI, metrics, heartbeat, thresholds, failure isolation and a blocked destination; real Slack/email delivery of an SMS alert is not tested.

The worker scans up to 100 jobs per page, skips locked jobs, updates a per-environment heartbeat only after a full pass, reconnects on DB errors and waits 30 seconds between scans. Monitor heartbeat age, failures and process health independently; scans are not proof that locked rows were evaluated and repeated history scans have untested costs. Event evidence is limited to callbacks bound to the current dispatch; dispatch-linked callbacks are checked against recorded account snapshots when present (see above), and older/custom callbacks may be unlinked. Duplicate-SID detection is snapshot-based and is reevaluated on later scans. Each known SID adds an indexed lookup; migration 0069 adds the index and associated write cost. Docker PostgreSQL tests synthetic records, a separate worker process, matching and conflicting SIDs, status changes, tenant isolation, row locks and retry cleanup. Real Twilio delivery/webhooks, API exposure of the result, missed callback reconciliation, load, and full HA remain untested or unimplemented. [Twilio recommends callback and periodic reconciliation](https://www.twilio.com/docs/messaging/guides/outbound-message-logging).

`NOVU_TWILIO_PROJECTION_DB_TIMEOUT_SECONDS` controls the Twilio projector's per-operation DB timeout, default 30 seconds, range 1–300. The worker also sets session-level PostgreSQL `statement_timeout` to the same value; use a direct connection or a session-affine pool, because transaction pooling can lose or leak this setting. Connection, environment check, each page, each individual projection and heartbeat write are timed separately; a whole scan can take longer. On timeout the connection driver is stopped and the worker reconnects after two seconds. Tune the threshold for expected DB latency: too low can repeatedly abort progress. An interrupted transaction may remain on the server until the server detects disconnection, especially during a network partition. Docker tests exercise a blocked heartbeat, timeout and reconnection, two worker processes, and a database-container restart. These are selected failure scenarios, not proof of production failover or network-partition recovery; monitor the heartbeat and process externally.

For the Resend worker, `NOVU_RESEND_API_KEY_FILE` can reference a mounted secret file instead of `NOVU_RESEND_API_KEY`. Setting both fails startup. By default the file is read once at startup. Set `NOVU_RESEND_RELOAD_API_KEY=true` to re-read and validate the file immediately before each job claim; this option requires the file source and rejects invalid settings. An invalid file at startup prevents startup; after startup, a missing, malformed or unreadable file pauses new claims and retries after two seconds; it does not silently dispatch with the previous key. Expired job recovery continues, and shutdown can interrupt the wait. Atomically replace the file to avoid exposing a partially written value. A request already in progress keeps the key it started with; different worker instances can observe a replacement at different times. A valid-looking key can still be rejected by Resend, and synchronous file access can stall on an unhealthy filesystem. Restrict file and process access and monitor worker logs and backlog. Other Resend consumers and webhook secrets do not use this reload setting, and no external secret manager integration is provided.

The adapter test sends two requests to a local mock and verifies the old and new authorization headers across an atomic file replacement. A Docker PostgreSQL integration test runs the actual worker, checks that an invalid file leaves a queued job pending and that a valid replacement resumes processing; its intentionally invalid job fails before any provider request. Live Resend rotation, multiple workers and external secret-store behavior have not been tested.

The SendGrid worker also supports opt-in file rotation: set `NOVU_SENDGRID_API_KEY_FILE` and `NOVU_SENDGRID_RELOAD_API_KEY=true`. Its startup validation, per-claim reload, two-second pause on a post-start read or format failure, atomic replacement guidance, in-flight request and multi-instance limitations match the Resend worker. Dispatch tracking and region settings remain unchanged. The SendGrid adapter test checks the two authorization headers with a local mock; a separate Docker PostgreSQL test checks the actual worker's pause and resume using a job that fails before provider traffic. Actual SendGrid rotation, multi-worker timing and external secret managers remain unverified. Alert commands using SendGrid are separate processes and do not use this worker setting.

The Twilio worker can instead use `NOVU_TWILIO_CREDENTIALS_FILE` for an atomic, reloadable credential pair. The file is a single-line UTF-8 JSON object, at most 16 KiB, with either `{"mode":"auth_token","auth_token":"..."}` or `{"mode":"api_key","api_key_sid":"...","api_key_secret":"..."}`. The account SID, sender and region remain separate startup settings. Do not combine this file with any `NOVU_TWILIO_AUTH_TOKEN`, `NOVU_TWILIO_AUTH_TOKEN_FILE`, `NOVU_TWILIO_API_KEY_SID`, `NOVU_TWILIO_API_KEY_SECRET` or `NOVU_TWILIO_API_KEY_SECRET_FILE` setting: conflicting sources fail startup. Without the new file, existing settings and startup-only behavior are unchanged.

With this file selected, the worker reads and validates it at startup and before each new claim; an invalid file after startup pauses claims and retries after two seconds while expired-job recovery continues. Replace the complete file atomically so an API key SID and secret cannot be read from different rotations. The file can switch between the two supported credential modes; the account SID does not change. A request already in progress retains its original credentials, and workers can observe the new file at different times. Provider rejection of a syntactically valid credential, synchronous filesystem stalls, file permissions and backlog monitoring remain operator concerns. Twilio webhook signature verification uses separate credentials and must be rotated separately and coordinated with the provider. Local mock HTTP checks both Basic authentication modes, and Docker PostgreSQL verifies actual worker pause/resume with a job that fails before external traffic; real Twilio rotation, callback coordination, external secret managers and multi-worker deployment remain unverified.

For the **standalone** `slack-worker`, set `NOVU_SLACK_BOT_TOKEN_FILE` and `NOVU_SLACK_RELOAD_BOT_TOKEN=true` to re-read the token before each new claim. It rejects invalid settings or conflicting sources at startup; after startup a missing, malformed or unreadable file pauses claims and retries after two seconds, while expired-job recovery continues. Atomically replace the file and protect it and process access. The worker validates token syntax only: the operator must ensure the replacement belongs to the intended Slack workspace and has the correct permissions. Existing in-flight requests and separate workers can use different tokens during rotation. This does not change the Slack workflow worker's registered multi-integration token map, webhook worker, or alert sender; those require their own coordinated configuration. A local HTTP mock checks the two authorization headers, and a Docker PostgreSQL test checks the real standalone worker's pause/resume using a job that fails before external traffic. Actual Slack rotation and multi-worker behavior remain unverified.

The FCM worker also supports mounted credentials: `NOVU_FCM_ACCESS_TOKEN_FILE` for a single-provider token, `NOVU_FCM_PRIVATE_KEY_FILE` for a multiline PKCS#8 PEM service-account key, and `NOVU_FCM_INTEGRATIONS_FILE` for the existing multi-integration JSON (which may be formatted across lines). Each replaces its same-named inline variable; setting both sources for the selected credential is an error. The integration map still takes precedence over single-provider settings, and within single-provider mode a configured access token takes precedence over service-account fields. Unselected settings are not checked. The token uses the shared 16 KiB single-line secret rules; the PEM is limited to 64 KiB and the JSON to 1 MiB, including when passed inline. Files must be nonempty UTF-8 without NUL. Malformed, unavailable or oversized files fail startup without printing file paths or secrets. By default credentials are read once; operators must protect file permissions, mounts and process memory. The optional fixed-token reload mode is described below. Static access tokens do not auto-refresh. Docker PostgreSQL tests worker startup with local fixture token, generated PEM and multi-integration JSON plus source conflicts and oversized input; no real FCM request or secret-manager integration is tested.

For a single fixed-token provider, `NOVU_FCM_RELOAD_ACCESS_TOKEN=true` opts into rereading `NOVU_FCM_ACCESS_TOKEN_FILE` immediately before each job claim. The default is false. This mode requires the file and rejects inline token, integration-map and service-account settings; the existing optional shared-provider policy remains available. The file must resolve to a regular file, at most 16 KiB with one optional trailing newline; symlinks to regular files support atomic secret-mount replacement. By default invalid or unavailable files pause new claims and are retried, while expired leases still go through normal recovery; the optional exit policy is described below. An already claimed job and its provider requests retain the token loaded for that job; rotation does not cancel them. Each worker reads its own file, so coordinate updates and token expiry across replicas. This mode does not obtain or refresh OAuth tokens, validate them with FCM, reload service-account keys or integration maps, or guarantee delivery; secure the mount and ensure valid tokens are supplied externally. Docker PostgreSQL tests invalid-file pause and atomic replacement resumption using a non-sendable job, local HTTP tests confirm old and new bearer headers, and Unix tests reject FIFO files. Actual FCM, secret-manager behavior, filesystem stalls and multi-worker rotation are unverified.

For multiple integrations, `NOVU_FCM_RELOAD_INTEGRATIONS=true` independently enables rereading `NOVU_FCM_INTEGRATIONS_FILE` before each claim. It requires a regular UTF-8 JSON file of at most 1 MiB and rejects inline integration, fixed-token and single-provider service-account credentials. The default is false; the two reload modes cannot be combined. A replacement must retain the exact integration ID-to-project mapping from startup, although fixed tokens and service-account credentials may change. The entire new map is validated before replacing the old one; by default malformed files, invalid keys or changed mappings pause new claims until repaired; the optional exit policy is described below. Unchanged file bytes reuse existing providers and cached OAuth tokens; a changed file rebuilds providers and their token caches even if only formatting changed. Recovery and already claimed jobs retain their existing behavior. Each replica reads independently; coordinate file delivery and credential expiry. Changing routing requires the existing coordinated restart and routing-guard procedure. The worker cannot prove new credentials belong to the intended identity or project without contacting FCM. Protect the file, process memory and error logs; no secrets or paths are emitted by the reload error. Docker PostgreSQL tests pause and recovery after a project mismatch, invalid service-account key and valid token/key replacement without external sends; Unix tests reject FIFO files. Real FCM OAuth, secret managers, multi-replica rotation, filesystem stalls and production load remain unverified.

For a single service account, set `NOVU_FCM_RELOAD_SERVICE_ACCOUNT=true` with `NOVU_FCM_PROJECT_ID`, `NOVU_FCM_CLIENT_EMAIL` and `NOVU_FCM_PRIVATE_KEY_FILE`. It is disabled by default and cannot be combined with integration-map, fixed-token or inline private-key settings or the other reload modes. Before each job claim the worker reads a regular UTF-8 PEM file up to 64 KiB; symlinks to regular files allow atomic replacement. A changed valid key replaces the service-account provider and resets its OAuth cache; unchanged bytes preserve the cache. By default an unavailable or invalid key pauses new claims until repaired while expired-job recovery continues; the optional exit policy is described below. Project and client email remain fixed until restart; already claimed jobs are unaffected. This validates local key format, not Google authorization or project identity. Each worker reads independently; coordinate rollout, file protection and key expiration. Docker PostgreSQL tests pause and resume using a non-sendable job and generated test keys; Unix FIFO rejection is also tested. Actual Google OAuth/FCM, secret managers, multi-worker rotation and filesystem stalls are unverified.

`NOVU_FCM_RELOAD_FAILURE_POLICY=pause|exit` chooses how any of the three reload modes handles a file read or validation failure. `pause` is the default and keeps retrying; `exit` terminates the worker with an error after closing its DB driver so a supervisor can report and restart it. `exit` requires an enabled reload mode; invalid values fail startup. A startup failure already exits in either policy. Neither policy starts a new claim after detecting the error, but an already claimed job is unaffected and can still have an unknown result. A supervisor may restart repeatedly while the file is invalid; configure backoff and alerting independently. This is not an in-process Slack/email alert and cannot detect a hang inside a filesystem read. Docker PostgreSQL tests corrupt each of the three credential file types after worker startup and verify error exit without printing secret values or paths; actual deployment supervisors, filesystem failures and external notifications are unverified.

Worker migration `0142` adds optional per-connection FCM credential reload observations. Set `NOVU_FCM_CREDENTIAL_HEALTH_ENABLED=true` on each participating worker and enable one reload mode. Register each worker DB login and current role OID for its environment in `novu_rust_fcm_routing_workers`, and grant that login EXECUTE on `public.novu_rust_fcm_credential_heartbeat(text,uuid,text)`. This registration is shared with, but does not require enabling, the FCM routing guard. Grant the trusted function owners `pg_read_all_stats` and the necessary table and helper-function privileges; use a protected NOLOGIN owner and schema because that role can view database activity metadata. Missing statistics privileges fail the check instead of silently reporting zero workers. The worker records status on change and at least every ten seconds while its loop progresses; observation failure prevents new claims and causes DB reconnect. A new DB connection uses a new instance ID. Stopped or disconnected sessions do not count as healthy even while their last observation is recent; a recent error remains unhealthy until it ages out. Old rows are not automatically deleted and require controlled retention.

Apply worker migration `0144` after `0142` to require the stored backend start time, as well as PID and login, to match when refreshing a credential observation. This prevents reuse of an old observation if a PID and instance ID are both reused. An unsuccessful heartbeat stops new claims and leads to reconnection with a new instance ID. The replacement function preserves explicit grants; manually reapplying `0142` restores the older check, so reapply `0144` afterward. A Docker PostgreSQL test simulates PID reuse by changing the recorded start time and confirms rejection, unchanged state, and preserved execution grants after reapplication; actual PID reuse and database failover remain unverified.

Worker migration `0145` offers optional, bounded retention of FCM observations; it does not schedule deletion. A DBA registers the operator's environment, LOGIN name and current role OID in `novu_rust_fcm_health_retention_operators` and grants that login EXECUTE on `public.novu_rust_prune_fcm_credential_health(text,integer,integer)`. The trusted NOLOGIN function owner needs schema USAGE, SELECT and UPDATE on the operator table, SELECT, UPDATE and DELETE on the health table, and membership in `pg_read_all_stats`; protect the function, tables and schema. Run, for example, `SELECT public.novu_rust_prune_fcm_credential_health('environment-id',604800,100);` as the registered login. The chosen age is 86401–31536000 seconds and the batch limit is 1–1000. Only older observations without a matching live PostgreSQL session are deleted; recent errors remain available to every supported health threshold. The function clears the caller's PostgreSQL statistics snapshot, locks the operator grant and skips locked rows. A zero result does not prove no eligible records exist if rows are locked. Deleting historical records changes stale counts and is irreversible; choose retention and scheduling to meet operational and audit requirements. Audit support requires migration `0146` below. Database restore, privileged writes, long-running calls, table growth while disabled and HA failover remain operator concerns. Docker PostgreSQL tests cover scoped grants and revocation, owner statistics permission, age and batch bounds, active and recent rows, environment isolation and locked rows; production contention and failover are unverified.

Worker migration `0146` records every successful retention call, including zero deletions, in the same transaction as the delete. The record contains environment, DB login and OID, age, batch limit, deletion count, optional reason and timestamp. Grant the existing prune function owner INSERT on `public.novu_rust_fcm_health_retention_audit` and USAGE on `public.novu_rust_fcm_health_retention_audit_id_seq` before use; missing audit access rolls the deletion back. Set an optional reason in the same transaction using `SELECT set_config('swallow.fcm_health_retention_reason','Routine retention',true);`, then invoke the prune function and commit. The trimmed reason is limited to 500 bytes and excludes control characters; it may be absent. Protect access to the audit table and reasons. For restricted lookup, grant the registered operator EXECUTE on `public.novu_rust_fcm_health_retention_audit_page(text,bigint,integer)`; its trusted NOLOGIN owner needs schema USAGE, SELECT and UPDATE on the operator table, and SELECT on the audit table. Use `SELECT * FROM public.novu_rust_fcm_health_retention_audit_page('environment-id',NULL,50);` and pass the last ID as `before_id` for the next page. The role must also remain registered for that environment. ID ordering is not commit ordering, pages are not a shared snapshot, and direct privileged DB changes, rejected attempts, historical calls and audit tampering are not recorded. Manual reapplication of `0145` restores unaudited deletion: reapply `0146` after it. Audit retention, external immutable audit, operational load and HA remain unimplemented or unverified. Docker PostgreSQL verifies restricted lookup, environment isolation, optional reason and deletion count, and rollback when audit insertion is denied.


`delivery-reconcile check-fcm-credentials ENV MAX_AGE_SECONDS MIN_HEALTHY` returns aggregate healthy, unhealthy, stale and minimum counts, with exit 2 if healthy is below the requested minimum or a recent error exists, 0 when healthy, and 1 on errors. Grant the monitor login environment-specific `alert_view` and EXECUTE on `public.novu_rust_fcm_credential_health_check(text,integer)`; direct table access is unnecessary. Set the age above normal job execution plus the ten-second interval and set a minimum consistent with replica count. Dead recent healthy rows are excluded from healthy and are not yet counted stale. An invalid file at startup before the first observation can only be inferred from a missing worker; workers without this option are invisible. Session pooling must preserve backend identity, and direct privileged edits, delayed network disconnect detection, long jobs, clock changes and independent database failures limit the observation. This CLI does not itself send Slack/email; schedule it and monitor its exit independently. Docker PostgreSQL tests use two real workers, one corrupted file, recovery, process termination, scoped access, role revocation and restricted function ownership. Real supervisors, Slack/email integration and production HA are unverified.
