To include this check in the existing Slack and/or email alert command, set both `SWALLOW_ALERT_FCM_CREDENTIAL_MAX_AGE_SECONDS` (1–86400) and `SWALLOW_ALERT_FCM_CREDENTIAL_MIN_HEALTHY` (1–1000) on `send-delivery-alert`; omit both to disable it. The alert login needs environment `alert_view` and EXECUTE on `public.novu_rust_fcm_credential_health_check(text,integer)`, as well as the existing destination permissions. An unhealthy count or insufficient healthy workers produces an alert; a check failure is itself alerted and exits 1, even if the destination is blocked or cooling down. It uses the existing destination reservation, unknown-outcome review and selected cooldown policy. FCM credential problems use a separate condition bit for `new-condition` cooldown; worker migration `0143` permits the signed high bit in the existing integer masks. Apply `0142` and `0143`, grant the required functions, and replace all alert senders that share a destination before enabling this check. Older senders do not understand this condition and can overwrite its cooldown state; manually reapplying older migrations `0088` or `0093` restores incompatible validation. The bitmask may be negative when this condition is active. Docker PostgreSQL tests use real FCM workers and both alert destination paths with blocked test reservations, check-failure handling, and direct/scoped cooldown recovery; no external Slack or email was sent. Real provider receipt, schedulers, multi-instance upgrades and production load remain unverified.

A separate function-level integration test reads FCM health from Docker PostgreSQL and verifies the alert bodies received by local mock Slack and Resend HTTP servers. It also checks that both destinations are contacted again when the condition recurs after recovery during cooldown. This test does not run the FCM worker or alert CLI and does not verify external provider receipt.

`src/resend.rs` provides a bounded Resend email HTTP adapter with optional idempotency keys and mock HTTP tests. The `resend-worker` consumes `experimental_resend_email` jobs. With migration `0061`, a workflow with `email_provider=resend` creates those jobs; the restricted experimental API can change that setting and the worker rechecks subscriber identity, preferences and workflow before rendering. Standalone manually prepared jobs have no such revalidation; integration management and automatic webhook state updates remain incomplete. Apply worker migrations through `0062`, set `DATABASE_URL`, `NOVU_RESEND_ENVIRONMENT_ID`, `NOVU_RESEND_API_KEY`, `NOVU_RESEND_FROM`, and optionally `NOVU_RESEND_SENDER_NAME`. Queue payloads contain recipient addresses and content; credentials are passed in the environment. Restrict both. The worker records each attempt before dispatch using `swallow/<job UUID>` as the idempotency key. Expired pre-attempt leases requeue; expired attempts, network failures, malformed success responses, HTTP 409 and 5xx remain unknown without automatic retry. Other non-2xx responses are rejected. `check`, `list`, and manual `retry`/`accepted` include these attempts; Resend manual retry requires migrations `0060` and `0063`. The operator must independently verify non-acceptance before retrying or acceptance before marking accepted. Resend retains idempotency keys for [24 hours](https://resend.com/docs/dashboard/emails/idempotency-keys); a later retry can duplicate mail. Direct byte attachments (including CID) and string headers are supported; attachments are limited to 20 MiB of raw data. Bounded workflow-trigger attachments are described above; provider custom data and disposition combinations that cannot be mapped to CID remain unsupported for Resend. Mock HTTP and disposable PostgreSQL cover a basic send and crash recovery; on 2026-11-14 a real adapter send first returned HTTP 403; a retry with a different sender domain was accepted by Resend. The user confirmed mailbox receipt; the limited live workflow and mocked original-server comparison are described below. Full Novu compatibility remains unverified. This standalone path is not production-ready.

An explicitly ignored `resend::tests::live_send` test sends a real message when `NOVU_RESEND_API_KEY`, `NOVU_RESEND_FROM` and `NOVU_RESEND_TEST_TO` are set. Run it only when an actual send is intended: `cargo test --locked --lib resend::tests::live_send -- --ignored`. It checks provider acceptance only, not mailbox receipt or the workflow path; each run generates a new idempotency key and may send another message.

`./scripts/test-resend-live.sh` also sends a real message and requires the same three environment variables plus Docker. It creates a disposable PostgreSQL container, inserts a trigger job directly, runs the Rust workflow and Resend workers, verifies provider acceptance and stored statuses, and removes the container. It does not run the API HTTP trigger, management API, webhook or original Novu, and it does not verify mailbox receipt. One successful run was performed on 2026-11-14 and the user confirmed receipt; each execution may send another message.

`cargo test --locked --test reference_resend_source -- --ignored` executes the original checked-out Resend provider's `sendMessage` with a mocked SDK and empty bridge data, comparing four selected option mappings to the Rust request. The test maps `replyTo`, attachment bytes and CID to HTTP fields itself; it does not exercise the original SDK serialization, full server, passthrough, the full attachment and header contract or webhooks. The checkout's provider source was verified against the pinned Novu commit.

With a built pinned source checkout and Docker, `NOVU_REFERENCE_SOURCE=/absolute/path cargo test --locked --test reference_resend_wire -- --ignored` runs the original provider and actual Node SDK with `fetch` mocked. Two selected request bodies match. For byte attachments the SDK serializes a JSON Buffer object, while Rust sends a Base64 string described by the API documentation: the raw request is different, and real attachment delivery has not been verified. Rust's queue also adds an idempotency header absent from the original default request. This comparison does not run the full Novu server.

The API harness `NOVU_TEST_PREFERENCE_DELIVERY=1 NOVU_TEST_SNOOZE_ONLY=1 NOVU_TEST_RESEND_ONLY=1 ./scripts/test-reference-subscribers.sh` (from `apps/api/rust-api`, with `NOVU_REFERENCE_SOURCE` set to a built pinned checkout) compares one trigger through both original and Rust API/workers to a mocked Resend request. The request body and selected `sent`/provider-ID history matched; Rust's additional delivery state was checked separately. It uses compatibility mode, mocks the provider and does not test real delivery, webhooks, management, attachments or all error paths. Idempotency headers are not compared.

The PostgreSQL fixture also checks 409, 429, 500 and malformed success responses, confirms failed jobs are not automatically dispatched a second time, and verifies unrecognized job fields fail before making a request. A separate strict-mode PostgreSQL fixture checks a workflow-generated Resend request, email history and the rejection of a replaced subscriber. These checks use a local mock; they do not establish actual Resend retry or delivery behavior.

The strict-mode fixture also races two workers for a single job and tests an expired lease while an HTTP request is held: recovery marks the outcome unknown and the stale worker cannot overwrite it after a late success. It does not establish full HA under database outages, network partitions, sustained load or real-provider failures.

`delivery-reconcile sms-delivery ENV JOB` derives a read-only `providerState` from stored Twilio events bound to the current dispatch ID, independently of the local `attemptState`. It reports `delivered`, `failed` (including `undelivered` and `canceled`), `pending`, `conflict`, `unrecognized` or `unavailable`. Conflicting terminal events or multiple message SIDs are never resolved automatically; events from another dispatch, environment or legacy callbacks without dispatch IDs do not count. Delivery is the provider's reported status, not proof of user reading or non-acceptance. Events can arrive later and a callback URL reused by another request can misattribute evidence; protect callback URLs and event table writes. This command does not update jobs, retry or establish real-provider behavior. Fixture tests do not exercise a real Twilio account.

Topic jobs in default safety mode require an acceptance-time `topicIdentitySnapshot` from the updated API. Recreated subscribers and members added after acceptance are skipped and the job is marked failed, although unchanged members may already have been processed. Legacy queued topic jobs without a snapshot fail safely. Upgrade the API first and drain or review old topic jobs before updating workers. Compatibility mode restores current-membership expansion and its ID-reuse risk. Snapshot size grows with the topic; large-scale performance and all concurrent cases remain unverified.

Worker migration `0068` installs `public.novu_rust_lock_operator_grant(text,text)` as a security-definer function, with a fixed `pg_catalog` search path and an explicitly qualified `public` grant table. Install it in a database using that schema as a trusted owner with permission to lock grant rows. The function owner and the `public` schema must be protected from untrusted modification. It checks only the caller's `session_user`, environment and decision, takes a shared row lock until the operation's database transaction ends, and has no public EXECUTE privilege. A database administrator must grant EXECUTE explicitly to each scheduler/operator login or its role, for example:

```sql
GRANT EXECUTE ON FUNCTION public.novu_rust_lock_operator_grant(text,text) TO "scheduler-login";
```

Grant rows still need to be provisioned independently; function execution does not grant an action. The caller does not need UPDATE or DELETE on the grants table. A committed revocation cannot overtake an already-locked database decision or reservation, and a new operation after revocation is denied. Revocation may wait for long transactions; a reservation committed before revocation can still start or finish its external request afterward. Old binaries do not take this lock, and privileged direct SQL bypasses the tool. Apply migrations and grant EXECUTE before upgrading; remove or stop old binaries to obtain the stated guarantee. Docker PostgreSQL verifies the lock and revocation using a restricted session; production role provisioning, lock timeouts and all network failures remain untested.

Manual `retry` and `accepted` decisions require worker migrations `0064` and `0068` and an explicit row in `novu_rust_delivery_operator_grants` matching the environment, PostgreSQL `session_user` and exact decision. There are no default grants, including for PostgreSQL superusers. Use the audited `operator-grant` command described above to bind the current role identity; grant only the required action and environment. New audit records contain the authenticated DB session user and effective `current_user`, plus the caller-supplied operator label and reason. Historical records have a null session user and are not backfilled. The caller needs execute access to the restricted grant-lock function described below; only administrators should change grant rows or audit records. This gate protects the reconciliation command, not arbitrary SQL issued by a principal that can directly modify queue, attempt or audit tables. Shared database credentials or connection pooling with a shared login do not identify individual operators. There is no user identity provider, HTTP grant management API or tamper-proof audit; the restricted DB grant management function and CLI require separate administrator bootstrap. Apply migration and provision grants before upgrading the command; old binaries do not enforce the gate and must be withdrawn.

`delivery-reconcile check ENV` emits JSON counts and oldest update timestamps for unknown attempts and dispatching attempts whose job is not running or whose lease has expired. Exit 0 means neither was found, 2 means operator attention is needed, and 1 indicates a command or database error. Counts are per attempt (FCM per target), without the list command's 100-row limit. Apply migration `0060`; the DB role needs read access to the environment table, queue and all four attempt tables. An environment absent from the environment table returns an error instead of a false healthy result. A valid but unintended environment is still possible. Monitor command failures and absence of execution separately. This snapshot can alert during normal lease recovery, scans attempt tables, and neither resends nor detects all backlog or delivery failures. Production performance and alert integration remain unverified.

`resend-projection-worker` periodically projects stored Resend evidence into `resend_delivery_result` on the queue job and linked email message. Apply API migrations through `0013` and worker migrations through `0065`, deploy the updated reconciliation tool before running the projector, and set `DATABASE_URL` and `NOVU_RESEND_ENVIRONMENT_ID`. Run it under a process supervisor and alert on absence or failures. It reads all Resend attempts for the environment in pages of 100 and repeats 30 seconds after each full scan; scan time and load grow with retained jobs and events. It locks one queue row with `SKIP LOCKED` and computes from a repeatable-read snapshot, then updates both results in the same transaction. Multiple projectors may repeat work; skipped rows are retried on the next scan. Outputs are `unknown`, `pending`, `delivered`, `failed`, `partial`, `conflicting`, or `complained`; null means not yet projected or reset on manual retry. A late event changes the result on a later scan. The existing queue `status`, message `status` and `delivery_state` still describe dispatch, not final delivery, and the new column is not returned by Novu message APIs. Unmatched events, old recipient snapshots, unknown IDs and conflicting evidence remain conservative. The snapshot is point-in-time: later callbacks or duplicate provider IDs can change a result. Database failures and scan or serialization errors cause a reconnect after two seconds and a fresh scan; a missing environment still stops the worker. A persistent schema, permission or database error also retries indefinitely, so process liveness alone is insufficient: monitor its error logs and separately check stale or unmatched evidence. Older reconciliation binaries do not clear the projection on retry; upgrade all of them before starting the projector. A disposable PostgreSQL test kills the projector, inserts later evidence, restarts it and checks that the result is refreshed. A real single-recipient Resend delivered webhook was accepted with HTTP 204 and projected as delivered in both the job and email; other real event types, multiple recipients, load, sustained failover and complete HA remain unverified. A disposable PostgreSQL restart test verifies one reconnect and later projection. PostgreSQL fixture tests do not establish production readiness.

`NOVU_RESEND_PROJECTION_DB_TIMEOUT_SECONDS` sets a per-operation database timeout, default 30 seconds and range 1–300. The worker sets the same session `statement_timeout`, so use a direct connection or session-affine pool; transaction pooling may lose or leak the setting. Connection, environment lookup, page lookup, each projection transaction and heartbeat are timed separately, not the entire scan. A timeout closes the connection and triggers a new scan after a two-second delay. Set the threshold for normal DB latency: overly short values can prevent progress. In a network partition, server-side transactions or locks can persist until the server detects the failure or its timeout expires. Docker PostgreSQL tests a blocked heartbeat, timeout and reconnection, two concurrent workers, a database restart and later synthetic delivery evidence; this does not establish production failover or full network-partition safety. Monitor worker execution and heartbeat independently.

`delivery-reconcile check-resend ENV GRACE_SECONDS HEARTBEAT_MAX_SECONDS` provides an environment-wide alert signal for monitoring systems. It exits 2 if a non-cancelled Resend job has been runnable longer than the grace period without a `delivered` projection, or if the projection worker has not completed a scan within the heartbeat threshold; it exits 0 otherwise and 1 on invalid configuration or database failure. Both thresholds must be 1–86400 seconds. Worker migration `0065` adds the per-environment scan heartbeat; the projector writes it only after completing a scan. A heartbeat is not proof that every job was examined: locked rows can be skipped and evidence may arrive after a scan. A process can appear healthy until the threshold expires; an empty environment still requires a running projector. Jobs cancelled before this check are excluded, and a provider-reported delivery does not prove mailbox reading. Large histories require repeated scans and writes to one heartbeat row per completed scan, with untested performance under load. Deploy the migration before the updated projector. Configure the external monitoring schedule, the notification destination, and alerts for exit code 1 and missing executions separately; this command sends no message.

`delivery-reconcile check-resend-metrics ENV GRACE_SECONDS HEARTBEAT_MAX_SECONDS` exposes the same snapshot as Prometheus text format gauges with an escaped environment label: `swallow_resend_overdue_undelivered`, `swallow_resend_projector_stale`, and `swallow_resend_eligible_jobs`. It exits 0 after any successful query, including an unhealthy result, so a textfile collector can publish the gauges; command/database errors exit 1. Configure collection and alert rules externally and monitor collection failures separately. The metrics output and JSON/exit-code check are alternatives that may be used together; neither chooses or configures a notification recipient. The separate alert command supports Slack and Resend email. Other direct destinations require external monitoring or future adapters.

`delivery-reconcile resend-check ENV JOB` evaluates the current signed Resend evidence for a single job and exits 0 only when the attempt is `accepted`, there is at least one recorded recipient, the provider ID is known and unique, every recipient is reported delivered without a complaint, and no event is unlinked to a recorded recipient. It exits 2 for an unknown or other non-accepted attempt, pending, rejected, conflicting, ambiguous, unlinked, complained, missing recipient snapshots, or unmatched-event evidence; command/database errors exit 1. This now shares the projection's classification; older binaries could report healthy for a non-accepted attempt with delivered evidence and must be replaced. The JSON output contains the assessment but not recipient addresses. Configure an external scheduler, alert destination, pending grace period, and job discovery; this command alone neither sends notifications nor monitors all jobs. Both `resend-check` and `resend-evidence` read their statements in one read-only repeatable-read transaction; callers of the lower-level `resend_evidence` function must supply their own transaction if they need the same guarantee. The result is a past snapshot, and events or decisions can change immediately afterward. A concurrent PostgreSQL test pauses the real CLI between queries and changes the attempt and event together, confirming the check does not combine different snapshots. This adds one short transaction per command; operational contention and provider races have not been tested. Exact-address mismatches and events lacking exactly one recipient can cause conservative alerts. API migrations 0010, 0012, 0013 and worker migrations 0060, 0062 and appropriate read privileges are required. One real Resend delivered event was checked successfully; other real events and an end-to-end alerting system have not been tested. The unknown-attempt correction is tested with synthetic events in disposable PostgreSQL, not a live provider race.

`delivery-reconcile check-queue ENV MAX_WAIT_SECONDS` counts runnable pending jobs overdue by the specified 1–86400 seconds and running jobs with expired leases, across all job kinds. Scheduled future jobs are excluded. It returns the same exit codes as `check`, includes the oldest eligible timestamps, and needs queue and environment-table read access. A nonexistent environment returns an error. Alert scheduling, failures, missing runs and validation that the chosen existing environment is the intended one must be configured separately. Normal lease recovery may cause transient alerts; choose a suitable threshold and alert delay. This detects some pre-dispatch stalls but cannot establish delivery or detect every lost job. Large-scale query performance is unverified.

This crate contains experimental Rust/PostgreSQL queue and worker implementations used by the Rust API. It is **not a fully Novu-compatible worker**. Do not deploy it as a replacement. The target is 100% compatibility with the selected Novu version, but this crate does not establish that compatibility.

The migration defines a queue table. The library exposes transactional enqueue (including delay), per-kind claim with `SKIP LOCKED`, lease renewal, token-fenced completion, failure and retry, pending-job cancellation scoped by environment, and explicit recovery of expired jobs. Claim and recovery policy are deliberately separate: automatically retrying every expired job could change Novu's delivery semantics or duplicate external side effects. The shared [`trigger_gate`](../../api/rust-contracts/src/trigger_gate.rs) module reproduces a small, explicitly bounded part of the stored-workflow trigger decisions from the reference source; it does not implement the full API or workflow engine.

Migration `0053` adds `delivery_state` to experimental email, SMS and push history: `not_attempted`, `dispatching`, `in_progress`, `accepted`, `rejected` or `unknown`. `accepted` means provider acceptance, not delivery. Expired in-flight attempts are reflected as `unknown`; existing historical rows without reliable attempts remain unknown. Novu-compatible `status` is retained separately and can still read `sent` without proof of delivery. This state is currently database-only; webhooks, actual delivery confirmation and alerting are not implemented. Each attempt adds history updates, and FCM target groups add an aggregation query.

Migrations `0054`, `0056` and `0057` provide an operator decision log and retry-dispatch identities. `cargo run --bin delivery-reconcile -- list ENV` lists up to 100 unknown attempts per page without payloads. Continue with `list ENV AFTER_JOB AFTER_TARGET`, using the last row’s job ID and target index (`-` for null). This is not a snapshot: repeat the scan to catch concurrent changes. `evidence ENV EMAIL_JOB` summarizes authenticated SendGrid events by type and distinct hashed recipient count; API migration `0004` and read access to its event table are required. These counts do not prove delivery to every intended recipient. After independently checking the provider, `cargo run --bin delivery-reconcile -- retry ENV JOB - OPERATOR TICKET` requeues one confirmed-unaccepted email/SMS attempt; use a numeric target index instead of `-` for FCM. Replace `retry` with `accepted` only after confirming provider acceptance. For SMS, `retry` refuses to requeue when a matching or ambiguous authenticated Twilio callback is already linked to the job; it does not change the job or record the refused decision. SMS retries require API webhook migrations through `0007` and permission to read the event table, even without automatic callback configuration. A different dispatch or environment alone does not block a known-dispatch retry; legacy job-only evidence or unknown dispatches block conservatively. Missing evidence does not prove non-acceptance. A wrong retry decision can duplicate a delivery; no provider verification is automated. Email/SMS retries re-read subscriber and preference data and may render different content. The tool requires privileged DB access, records the supplied operator, DB user and reason, and serializes conflicting decisions; the operator name itself is not authenticated. Restrict DB and audit-table update/delete privileges separately and do not put secrets or PII in the reason. After the last unknown FCM attempt is accepted, a job failed solely for uncertainty resumes any unattempted prepared targets; other failure reasons are preserved, and retries are refused for them. Standalone FCM acceptance may leave the job failed, and actual provider testing, comprehensive delivery confirmation and production operator RBAC are incomplete.

With worker migrations `0057`–`0058` and API migrations `0008`–`0009`, set `NOVU_SENDGRID_DISPATCH_TRACKING=true` to include a random `swallow_dispatch_id` and independent random bearer proof `swallow_dispatch_proof` in SendGrid `custom_args` for each request. Only the SHA-256 proof hash is retained in PostgreSQL. Tracking is off by default to preserve original request bodies. A user-supplied value for either reserved key causes the tracked send to fail before dispatch. The extra field counts against SendGrid custom-argument limits. A UUID alone cannot authenticate a dispatch. Treat the proof as a secret: it is sent to SendGrid and through the webhook, and a party that obtains both ID and proof can replay them. Protect provider access, transport and logs. Signed events are linked only when that ID and proof match a dispatch registered for the same job and environment; missing, malformed or mismatched IDs or proofs stay unbound. Pre-upgrade events are treated as unverified even if they already contain an ID; replay of the same event ID does not retroactively verify them. `delivery-reconcile email-dispatch-evidence ENV JOB` groups up to 100 event/dispatch pairs without revealing recipient hashes and errors if there are more. The previous `evidence` command remains aggregated. SendGrid retry decisions and the email worker check authenticated, job-linked webhook evidence. For tracked jobs, matching or unbound recipient events block a full retry; unrelated tracked dispatches do not. Legacy/unbound events can conservatively block later attempts. An event does not prove all recipients received the email. The API locks associated jobs in ID order while recording a signed batch, serializing with these checks. Upgrade every API instance before the worker and reconciliation tool; older APIs do not take these locks and older workers do not honor proof verification. Suspend retries until all instances have been upgraded. Apply API migrations through `0009` and worker migrations through `0058`; the email worker and reconciliation DB roles need read access to the event and audit tables, and the API role needs queue row-lock privileges. Missing/deleted message links, overridden message IDs, callbacks arriving after the dispatch check, absent events and actual SendGrid behavior remain unresolved. SendGrid may retain custom arguments long-term; the UUID and bearer proof require a retention policy and strict access control. A refused command is not separately audited.

An experimental worker loop supports the limited in-app and Inbox prototype described in the [API README](../../api/rust-api/README.md). It atomically creates simple subscriber and message records and completes a job; complete Novu Inbox compatibility is not established. On startup and at most every five seconds, this worker requeues expired leases **only for its in-app job kind**; other job kinds still require an explicit recovery policy. A malformed job or an input unsupported by the experimental renderer is recorded as failed without storing the payload in the diagnostic, and the worker continues. Database failures still terminate the process and require an external restart; there is no attempt limit for crash recovery. This is an experimental safety policy, not a verified reproduction of Novu's job failure or retry behavior. The limited interpolation implementation can be checked against the reference `handlebars@4.7.10` by installing that package in a temporary directory and running `NODE_PATH=/path/to/node_modules cargo test --test reference_template -- --ignored`. There is no scheduler process, cancellation of running work, generic queue deduplication, general retry/backoff or complete workflow integration with external providers. In particular, a lease cannot prevent an already-started external call from completing after lease expiry. Consumers must decide their side-effect and recovery policy. The migrations are initial development artifacts, not a production migration system; run them only on an isolated database.

Overlapping in-app batches acquire transaction-scoped advisory locks in sorted hash-key order before subscriber row locks, preventing opposite recipient order deadlocks between these workers while preserving recipient processing order. This adds one query per job and one lock per distinct subscriber hash; collisions can serialize unrelated work. If a lease expires while waiting or processing, the worker rolls the transaction back and continues; periodic recovery can requeue the job. Queue completion, failure, retry and renewal validate the lease after taking the row lock. Repeated expiration under sustained contention remains possible; throughput, concurrent behavior and the retry policy have not been compared with Novu.

Run static checks with `cargo test` and `cargo clippy --all-targets -- -D warnings`. To launch a disposable PostgreSQL 17 Docker container, run `./scripts/test-postgres.sh`; it binds a random loopback port and removes the container on exit. The script runs both the queue contract and a test that starts the actual in-app worker to exercise lease recovery, rollback and failure isolation.

Alternatively, the integration test deliberately **drops and recreates** `novu_queue_jobs`; run it only against a disposable PostgreSQL database:

```sh
TEST_DATABASE_URL='postgresql://USER:PASSWORD@localhost:5432/DISPOSABLE_DATABASE' \
  cargo test --test postgres -- --ignored
```

The integration test is ignored by default so an unset database is not mistaken for a successful database test. For architectural context and compatibility requirements see [the design record](../../../RUST_POSTGRES_DESIGN_KO.md).

The in-app worker also processes `experimental-in-app-unsnooze` jobs with `subscriberId` and `notificationId` payload fields. It locks the subscriber, clears the snooze, makes the message unread, appends the delivery timestamp and completes the job atomically. Manual unsnooze cancels one matching pending job. The experimental API creates these jobs; reference retries and execution details remain unsupported. Each new in-app message or automatic unsnooze attempts to add an event to `0030_experimental_ws_events.sql` in the same transaction. `0031_experimental_ws_best_effort.sql` drops event-write failures without rolling back the message, matching the reference queue's best-effort policy. The experimental WS service can deliver a limited notification and counts from these events; reliable replay is not implemented. The worker checks for due unsnooze jobs before in-app jobs and performs an additional orphan-job query when none are due.

The single-step prototype uses migration `0033_experimental_in_app_step_active.sql` to distinguish an existing inactive step from the absence of steps. New-format jobs examine the current flag and `has_steps` at processing time; with no active step they update the subscriber but create no in-app message or step outcome. Older jobs retain their existing behavior. Migration `0034_experimental_notifications.sql` adds a partial notification record even without an active step; the full reference notification model remains incomplete.

The worker inserts one experimental notification per accepted recipient, including when the single in-app step is inactive or channel preferences suppress delivery. New messages refer to that notification; subscriber deletion leaves notification history. The reference comparison covers selected fields, active and inactive steps, message linkage and subscriber deletion. The table does not implement topics, workflow-run API, all reference fields, legacy-data backfill or full failure semantics; notification/message/job completion are committed together in Rust, unlike the original multi-stage workflow. Each recipient adds a notification insert and an index write.

`src/sendgrid.rs` provides an experimental standalone SendGrid HTTP adapter for HTML email, including global/EU endpoints, basic recipient lists, sender, reply-to, headers and Novu tracking arguments. The original provider wire body is compared in `tests/reference_sendgrid.rs`; local HTTP tests cover one-shot submission, 429 and redirects. It is connected to the experimental environment-scoped queue consumer and the limited manually configured email workflow step described below, but not to integration management or Novu delivery status. Dynamic template data, string alternatives and byte-array attachments are supported by the adapter, with selected request bodies compared to the original provider. Bounded trigger attachment handling is described above; arbitrary alternative binary content, provider passthrough and complete Novu-compatible retry and recovery are unsupported; no real provider delivery has been tested. Never treat a transport error as proof that a message was not accepted. Run the reference comparison with `NOVU_REFERENCE_SOURCE=/path/to/built/reference cargo test --locked --test reference_sendgrid -- --ignored`.

`src/twilio.rs` is an experimental SMS adapter supporting US/EU endpoints, account-token or API-key Basic authentication and form-encoded to/from/body. Three cases compare the built original provider's endpoint, request fields, credentials and response; a local mock exercises both authentication modes, 201, 429 and redirect rejection. Run `NOVU_REFERENCE_SOURCE=/path/to/built/reference cargo test --locked --test reference_twilio -- --ignored` for the original comparison.

`src/fcm.rs` provides experimental FCM message planning and a bounded HTTP v1 sender using a caller-provided OAuth access token or service-account credentials. Seven comparisons check the original provider's Firebase message plans; selected cases compare the original firebase-admin's validated HTTP message conversion. Local HTTP tests check token-by-token multicast submission and 200/429 results. Limited manually configured workflow and queue processing are described below; FCM-only subscriber credentials are described below; integration management APIs are not connected. Service-account OAuth uses an RS256 assertion, the original Firebase Admin scope set and token endpoint, and caches tokens until five minutes before expiry; concurrent refreshes are serialized. Original google-auth-library claims and a local OAuth-to-FCM mock were compared using generated test keys. Private keys and tokens remain in process memory; credential rotation, real Google OAuth, full Android/APNs/WebPush options, exact multicast result/error behavior, retries and real FCM delivery are not implemented or verified. Unsupported platform fields are rejected at HTTP conversion. Multicast sends sequentially, one request per token; a transport failure is ambiguous. Run `NOVU_REFERENCE_SOURCE=/path/to/built/reference cargo test --locked --test reference_fcm --test reference_fcm_wire -- --ignored` for the provider source comparisons. The OAuth comparison also requires OpenSSL: `NOVU_REFERENCE_SOURCE=/path/to/built/reference cargo test --locked --lib google_oauth::tests::original_google_auth_claims_match -- --ignored`.

`fcm-worker` consumes `experimental_fcm_push` jobs; all jobs require worker migrations through `0054` in order. Configure `DATABASE_URL`, `NOVU_FCM_ENVIRONMENT_ID`, `NOVU_FCM_PROJECT_ID`, and either `NOVU_FCM_ACCESS_TOKEN` or `NOVU_FCM_CLIENT_EMAIL` plus PKCS#8 PEM `NOVU_FCM_PRIVATE_KEY`. The job payload uses the snake_case `Push` fields: `title`, `content`, `targets`, `payload`, `overrides` and `provider_data`; it contains device tokens and content but no provider credentials. The worker records one attempt before each target request, requeues remaining targets after each result and marks an expired dispatch as unknown rather than automatically sending it again. If any target is unknown the job fails even when other targets succeeded; without unknowns, one acceptance completes the job, while all rejections fail it. A crash after recording an attempt but before requesting FCM can lose that target. Docker PostgreSQL and mock HTTP tests cover competing workers, isolation, mixed/all rejection and recovery; these queue semantics have not been compared with original Novu or real FCM. Workflow creation, message history, complete invalid device-token cleanup and full provider outcomes remain incomplete. Each target adds a queue lease, attempt insert and result transaction.

Migration `0046` introduced one manually configured FCM workflow step and subscriber device-token row. The trigger creates a recipient job; at dispatch it rechecks subscriber, workflow and selected push preferences, reads stored devices, renders supported title and body variables, and prepares one FCM request and independent message ID per device. Prepared requests are persisted before dispatch and reused on recovery. Four original-server cases compare one and two devices, missing devices and disabled push through mocked FCM; independently generated IDs are checked only for format. Later changes add limited subscriber credentials, message history, invalid-token cleanup and multiple integrations as described below. Full device registration and integration management APIs, template/override behavior, all queued changes and original retry semantics remain incomplete or unverified. Changes after preparation do not update already prepared targets. This adds a job and outcome insert per recipient, a subscriber query and lock per preparation, an ID query per target and storage for the prepared requests.

Migration `0047` initially adds one manually configured FCM integration identity per environment (extended by `0051`) and connects the bounded subscriber credentials API to the device row. `PUT` replaces tokens, `PATCH` appends unique tokens in order, and an empty array clears tokens; `DELETE` removes the FCM channel. The reference harness compares these operations and v1/v2 individual subscriber responses before triggering FCM delivery. An experimental FCM metadata API is documented in the API README; the Novu integration management contract, full selection, other credential fields and providers, full validation/error details and broader timestamp behavior remain incomplete or unverified. Selected no-op credential updates and FCM channel data in v2 lists are compared with the reference. Before preparing delivery the worker checks that the registered integration identity still exists and is active; legacy manually inserted device rows without an integration ID bypass this check. Subscriber deletion cascades the device row, and each individual subscriber response performs an additional channel query.

FCM workflow delivery checks `Requested entity was not found` in the message generated by the pinned Firebase SDK error conversion. Unknown JSON errors and non-JSON errors include the raw response in that message, so unrelated data containing the phrase can cause the same false-positive token removal as the reference. When `IS_EXPIRED_TOKENS_REMOVAL_ENABLED=true`, the worker removes that token only from the original environment, subscriber internal ID and integration ID; the default is disabled. The reference harness compares mocked invalid-token behavior with the flag enabled and disabled, including mixed valid and invalid tokens and preservation after a generic error when enabled. Sixteen JSON and non-JSON cases are compared against the pinned Firebase SDK and Novu provider using `NOVU_REFERENCE_SOURCE=/path/to/built/reference cargo test --locked --lib original_firebase_error_classification_matches -- --ignored`. Error bodies over 16 KiB, other SDK conversion cases, integration changes and other concurrent mutations during delivery, legacy jobs and real FCM failures are unsupported or unverified. Raw provider error bodies are not stored. Unlike the reference, cleanup is part of the fenced result transaction: a database error can fail that transaction, and cleanup is not retried after a lease loss. Removal adds a device-array and subscriber-timestamp update.

`tests/reference_push_race_compare.py` holds mocked FCM responses while changing tokens or deleting and recreating a subscriber, then compares cleanup with the original. Five selected cases match with cleanup enabled. Recreating the subscriber prevents the old attempt from removing its tokens, while re-registering the same token on the same subscriber does not protect it from removal. The comparison covers one integration and one target; it does not establish behavior under arbitrary concurrency or with a real provider.

Migration `0095` adds an opt-in cooperative FCM routing guard. With `SWALLOW_FCM_ENFORCE_ROUTING_CONFIG=true`, a worker claims an environment-scoped session advisory lock and registers a SHA-256 hash of its mode and sorted integration-ID-to-project-ID map; a different participating configuration rejects startup. The hash excludes access tokens, private keys and service-account identities, so rotation is possible but mismatched or invalid credentials are not detected. The default is disabled. Grant the worker DB login `EXECUTE` on `public.novu_rust_claim_fcm_routing(text,text)` and register its current login name and role OID in `novu_rust_fcm_routing_workers` for the environment. Protect the function owner, public schema and tables. To change routing, stop all participating workers, register a scoped grant administrator, grant it execution on `public.novu_rust_reset_fcm_routing(text,text)`, and call it with environment and reason. A live guard lock causes reset to return false; successful reset is audited. Reconnects reacquire the guard. Unprotected or old workers, direct database owners, secret identity changes, in-flight sends, revocation of already connected workers and actual FCM are outside this guarantee. The hash can reveal guessable project names; restrict its access.

The optional `SWALLOW_FCM_ALLOW_SHARED_PROVIDER=true` lets the single-provider configuration send for multiple integration IDs using the same FCM project and credential. The default is `false`; other values and combining `true` with an integration map reject startup. Use a per-integration map for different projects. Project equivalence is not checked automatically. A strict single-provider worker rejects prepared jobs with multiple recorded integration IDs before its next send, including jobs prepared by another configuration. Earlier targets may already be accepted; old jobs without per-target metadata cannot be checked this way. Keep every worker in an environment consistently configured and review queued or in-flight jobs before changes. Actual FCM delivery and all mixed-configuration or failure scenarios remain unverified.

Migration `0051` supports multiple manually configured FCM integrations and channel-specific device tokens. Set `NOVU_FCM_INTEGRATIONS` to a JSON object keyed by integration internal ID; each value contains `project_id` and either `access_token` or both `client_email` and PKCS#8 PEM `private_key`. Credentials remain in process environment and memory; this is not a production secret manager. The worker snapshots per-target integration and channel tokens, and refuses the entire job before sending if any active target lacks configured credentials. Existing `NOVU_FCM_PROJECT_ID` single-provider configuration rejects multiple channels by default; the explicitly selected shared option above supports them when they use the same credential. The original-server harness compares registration, reads, three mocked sends with distinct test authorization headers, selected message history, disabled-integration selection, per-integration invalid-token cleanup, missing-credential behavior and a worker restart between targets. Full routing overrides, conditions, integration management, credential rotation, all error/retry semantics, legacy queued jobs and real FCM are incomplete or unverified. The mapped mode requires integration metadata and does not send standalone or older prepared jobs without it. A multi-channel preparation adds one channel query and stores per-target routing metadata in PostgreSQL.

For workflow triggers, legacy `overrides.fcm` accepts a nonempty `deviceTokens` string array, string-valued `data`, and `type` of `data` or `notification`. For an eligible channel with registered tokens, it replaces the per-channel targets and applies the data to the FCM message; overrides are persisted in the trigger and push queue payloads. The original-server harness compares target replacement, notification data and data-only requests and selected history against mocked FCM. Migration `0052` adds a manually configured `push_step_id`; global `providers.fcm` and the matching step's provider override support string-valued `data`, notification `title`, `body`, `imageUrl`, and those fields inside `_passthrough.body`. Global and step layers merge in order, then passthrough is applied last. The original comparison covers conflicting data and title/body values and an unrelated step, but not `imageUrl`. Provider-wide `token` routes send once per populated active subscriber integration; `topic`, `condition`, and subscribers with no populated channels use the newest active integration. Five single-integration original-server cases compare these routes, step precedence and passthrough precedence; three multi-integration cases compare token fan-out, topic selection and token fallback, using mocked requests and selected history. Multiple token-route integrations require either the mapped worker configuration or explicit shared-provider mode. Conditional integration selection, equal creation times and absence of active integrations are not fully compared. Integration and bridge overrides, platform options, empty target arrays, other data types, and complete multi-integration and error/concurrency behavior remain unsupported or unverified; real FCM has not been tested. Direct routes add one active-integration query and store target metadata in the queue. Apply migrations through `0054`; existing workflows need a step ID configured to use step overrides.

Provider-wide and step `tokens` routes accept 1–500 nonempty strings and send once per eligible integration, or use the newest active integration when there is no populated channel. Each token is sent as a separate sequential HTTP request, while one message ID and history record are shared per integration. Original-server comparisons cover global, step and passthrough routing with successful mocked responses and an all-rejected case. Selected partial success, restart between targets and two-integration cases were compared using mock providers; crashes during dispatch and real FCM remain unverified; unknown outcomes result in an error even if another token was accepted, unlike the original SDK aggregation. The history is provisional until all group results are recorded. Group result aggregation adds a database query per target, in addition to per-target dispatch transactions.

For example, the value of `NOVU_FCM_INTEGRATIONS` can have this shape, with actual secrets supplied by the deployment environment:

```json
{
  "integration-internal-id-1": {"project_id": "project-a", "access_token": "<oauth-token-a>"},
  "integration-internal-id-2": {"project_id": "project-b", "client_email": "service@example.test", "private_key": "<PKCS8-PEM>"}
}
```

Migration `0048` adds partial per-target push message history. A message is inserted immediately before each attempt and updated with the message ID and accepted/error status after the response; `STORE_NOTIFICATION_CONTENT=true` stores rendered content. The original-server comparison covers one and two devices, a mock provider error, missing devices and disabled preferences with content storage off and on. For each system it checks that request and history IDs agree, without comparing independently generated IDs between systems. Provider error text is replaced with a fixed diagnostic, so it differs from the source. Older queued jobs without history metadata create no message, and a post-dispatch lease loss can leave `sent` history unreconciled. Subscriber deletion removes experimental channel history by internal subscriber ID with indexes from migration `0049`; the full message API, fields, retention, other deletion cases, webhooks and actual delivery remain incomplete or unverified. Each target adds a message insert and result update.

The queued-push original-server comparison checks changes before dispatch preparation: subscriber profile and devices, preferences, subscriber deletion and recreation, actor and context, template, and workflow deletion and recreation. Five further cases directly mutate integration fixtures to test deactivation, deletion, replacement, reactivation, and deactivation with empty tokens. It compares mocked FCM requests and selected results, including the absence of requests where applicable. In the tested recreation case the original and Rust compatibility mode send to the new subscriber's device while retaining the old internal subscriber ID in job and message history; reusing external subscriber IDs can expose queued notifications to a new person. This queued-change comparison covers one integration; the separate `0051` comparison covers selected multi-integration scenarios. Other changes after dispatch starts, original cache and retry behavior, and real FCM delivery remain unverified. Integration validation adds an existence lookup to the existing subscriber query.

Apply API migrations through `0007` and worker migrations through `0056`. Optionally set `NOVU_TWILIO_STATUS_CALLBACK_BASE_URL` to the exact same canonical HTTPS URL as the API's `NOVU_TWILIO_WEBHOOK_URL`. The worker commits a unique dispatch ID before each request and appends `/jobs/{job-uuid}/attempts/{dispatch-uuid}` unless a message or passthrough specifies its own callback. The ID remains after a manual retry removes the current attempt. The API validates the signed URL, job, environment and dispatch ID; `delivery-reconcile sms-evidence ENV JOB` reports the dispatch ID, or null for legacy job-only callbacks. It returns an error for more than 100 events and does not decide acceptance or recipient delivery. Legacy or explicitly overridden callbacks may not identify the dispatch. The API database role needs read access to the queue, attempt, dispatch-ID and reconciliation tables. Deleting a job removes its dispatch IDs; later callbacks cannot be linked. When automatic callbacks are configured, the worker checks linked signed evidence under the queue-row lock before dispatching a manually retried job. Existing evidence stops the retry and restores an unknown attempt for operator review. New retry decisions record the retried dispatch ID, and the guard checks only the latest retry decision against that dispatch. Older decision records or job-only events lack this ID, so they can still conservatively block a later retry. Restored unknown attempts retain the audited dispatch ID when available. The worker needs read access to the webhook events and reconciliation tables. Callbacks arriving after the check commits cannot stop the request; callbacks without a job link and workers without automatic callback configuration are not protected. An incorrect retry can still duplicate delivery. Real Twilio delivery and callbacks, retention, replay protection, multi-account configuration, and automatic reconciliation remain unverified or unimplemented. Apply both migrations and deploy the updated API to every API instance before upgrading workers, so callbacks do not reach an old API without the new route. Each dispatch adds one ID row and index entry.

The Twilio worker also accepts mounted `NOVU_TWILIO_AUTH_TOKEN_FILE` and `NOVU_TWILIO_API_KEY_SECRET_FILE` instead of the corresponding direct variables. Each secret must use at most one source; invalid or conflicting sources fail startup. Files are read once at startup, limited to 16 KiB and have one final newline removed. Choose account-token or API-key credentials according to the existing Twilio configuration. File permissions, rotation and an external secret manager remain deployment responsibilities. Disposable PostgreSQL tests verify fixture-file startup and conflict rejection; they do not send real SMS.

`twilio-worker` consumes `experimental_twilio_sms` jobs after applying all worker migrations through `0056` in order. Configure `DATABASE_URL`, `NOVU_TWILIO_ENVIRONMENT_ID`, `NOVU_TWILIO_ACCOUNT_SID`, `NOVU_TWILIO_FROM`, either `NOVU_TWILIO_AUTH_TOKEN` or both `NOVU_TWILIO_API_KEY_SID` and `NOVU_TWILIO_API_KEY_SECRET`, and optionally `NOVU_TWILIO_REGION=eu` (default `us`). Standalone payload fields are `to`, `content` and optional `from`; no credentials belong in payloads. The worker records an attempt before sending. An expired lease is requeued only before that record exists; otherwise the outcome is unknown and is not retried automatically. A crash between the record and actual request can therefore lose a send. PostgreSQL and mock HTTP tests cover acceptance, rejection, malformed responses, competing consumers, environment isolation and both recovery paths. Integration management, full passthrough, complete status webhooks and original-compatible retries remain unimplemented. No real Twilio delivery has been tested, and transport or malformed-response errors can be ambiguous. Each dispatch adds an attempt insert and result update.

Migration `0042` adds a manually configured single SMS step and workflow preference. An active SMS workflow creates jobs per recipient; at dispatch the consumer rechecks the subscriber, workflow and selected preferences, chooses the first nonblank override/payload/subscriber phone, renders supported template expressions and applies SMS `to`, `from` and `content` overrides. Migration `0043` records a partial message history with missing-phone warning, provider ID and provider error status. The original-server harness compares three mock Twilio requests, a 429 rejection, missing-phone and disabled-preference cases and selected message fields with content storage both disabled and enabled. The Rust provider diagnostic is redacted and differs from the original. `STORE_NOTIFICATION_CONTENT=true` stores rendered content and was compared in those six cases. Older queued jobs without message metadata do not create history. Expired post-dispatch leases and invalid jobs can leave a message marked `sent`; recovery does not reconcile it. Novu management and message APIs, retention, execution order, provider and step overrides, integration selection, broad template semantics, actor/context changes, retries and full error contracts are incomplete or unverified. The experimental outcome remains `queued` after a send; consult queue and attempt state for the result. Each recipient adds a job and outcome insert, with a subscriber query and lock at dispatch, a message insert and result update, and extra actor/context lookups when present.

Migration `0044` adds an SMS step identifier for a bounded Twilio override subset: `to`, `from`, `content`, `body`, `statusCallback`, `applicationSid` and `messagingServiceSid`, plus the Twilio SDK message options for pricing, feedback, delivery, retention, scheduling, media and content, including supported fields in provider `_passthrough.body`. Channel, legacy provider, global provider and step provider values are layered in source order; passthrough can override the final provider request and blank destinations do not replace the resolved number. Four additional original-server cases compare legacy, conflicting step/passthrough, blank destination and typed options including repeated form keys with content storage enabled. A passthrough recipient can differ from the number recorded in message history, as in the original. Other value types and casing transformations, header/query passthrough, integration-specific selection and general nested merge behavior remain unsupported or unverified. Overrides are stored in PostgreSQL jobs.

The queued-SMS original-server comparison pauses the source standard queue, then checks ten cases including phone/profile and preference changes, subscriber deletion and recreation, actor/context changes, template changes and workflow deletion/recreation. In the tested recreation case, the original and Rust compatibility mode send to the new subscriber's phone while retaining the old internal subscriber ID in the job and message history; reusing external subscriber IDs can expose previously queued notifications to a new person. Changes after dispatch begins, actor/context deletion or recreation, concurrent mutations, all cache/feature-flag modes and real provider delivery remain unverified.

`sendgrid-worker` consumes only `experimental_sendgrid_email` jobs for `NOVU_SENDGRID_ENVIRONMENT_ID`. Apply all worker migrations in order for workflow-produced jobs; standalone jobs require queue migration `0001_jobs.sql`, `0035_experimental_sendgrid_attempts.sql`, `0037_experimental_email_messages.sql`, and `0038_experimental_email_provider_result.sql`; configure `DATABASE_URL`, `NOVU_SENDGRID_API_KEY`, `NOVU_SENDGRID_FROM`, `NOVU_SENDGRID_SENDER_NAME` and optionally `NOVU_SENDGRID_REGION=eu` (default `global`). Queue payloads use the snake_case fields in `sendgrid::Email`; the experimental workflow processor creates them for configured email steps. Credentials are supplied through the process environment rather than queue payloads or the database.

The worker writes a dispatch marker before each external request. On lease expiry, jobs without a marker are requeued, while marked attempts become `unknown` and are not automatically resent. 2xx is recorded as provider acceptance, 4xx as rejection and transport/5xx as unknown; acceptance is not proof of delivery. PostgreSQL plus a local mock verifies these states, two-worker claiming, tenant separation and crash-boundary recovery. This conservative policy is not compared with Novu's retry behavior; provider webhooks, operator reconciliation, production secret management, full multi-tenant integration configuration and full workflow execution remain unimplemented. The worker can still lose a delivery when it crashes after recording the marker and before sending, and message data in jobs is stored in PostgreSQL. Per dispatch it adds a marker transaction and a result transaction; recovery locks up to 100 expired jobs per pass.

Migration `0036_experimental_email_step.sql` adds manually configured `email_subject`, `email_html`, `email_step_active`, and workflow email preference fields. A trigger for an active configured email-only workflow, or a workflow also containing the experimental in-app step, is expanded by the existing processor. It creates one SendGrid job per recipient in the workflow transaction and evaluates limited global/workflow/subscriber email preferences and renders supported variables during delivery. Missing email and preference suppression are recorded as experimental outcomes. The queue holds the template and workflow data, and the worker process receives its SendGrid key from environment variables. Configuration requires direct database setup; the Novu workflow and integration APIs do not configure it. Email-only and two-channel workflows, execution order, message history, layouts, advanced templates, preferences and error handling are not fully compatible. Before dispatch, new workflow email jobs recheck subscriber identity and email preferences and rerender with the current profile; changes after dispatch starts cannot cancel an in-flight request. No actual external send has been performed. The original-server differential harness compares two custom HTML workflows, including one CSS rule, from trigger through mocked SendGrid request; independently generated message IDs are checked only for format and consistency. The source email compiler comparison covers three simple custom HTML cases without layouts. Rust uses css-inline while the reference uses inline-css, so broader HTML/CSS equivalence remains unproven; remote stylesheets are not fetched. This adds one queue insert and one outcome insert per eligible email recipient, plus an outcome insert for skipped recipients, in addition to existing workflow queries.

Migration `0037_experimental_email_messages.sql` records partial email history for preference-eligible recipients. A missing email creates a warning record with the same selected fields observed in the original; a preference cancellation creates none. For queued mail, `sent` is the original message model's initial value and does not mean the provider accepted or delivered the message. The stored message ID is passed to the provider job. The original-server comparison covers selected fields for missing address and preference cancellation, not full message API, history retention, deletion, content storage policy, or all provider-error details. Each eligible recipient adds an object-ID query and a message insert plus an index write.

Migration `0038_experimental_email_provider_result.sql` allows a provider error status and identifier in partial email history. The SendGrid consumer writes the result and matching environment-scoped message update in the same lease-fenced transaction: acceptance sets the returned identifier, while provider or transport errors set `error` and a fixed diagnostic. A 429 mock was compared with the original API and worker; status and error ID match, but Rust intentionally does not persist the raw provider response, so the error text differs. A transport or 5xx outcome can still be externally accepted despite the local error state. If the worker loses its lease after dispatch or an invalid job fails before dispatch, message status may remain `sent`; recovery does not reconcile it. Full retry, provider webhook and original job lifecycle semantics remain unimplemented. The result transaction adds one environment- and job-scoped message update per processed email.

The experimental trigger passes a limited `overrides.email` subset to the email worker: `toRecipient`, nonempty address arrays, explicit nonempty replacement `to`, sender and reply fields, subject, HTML/text, headers and custom data. The original-server comparison covers selected append, deduplication and replacement cases through mocked provider requests. Empty replacement `to`, integration selection, IP pools, full provider/step-scoped overrides and full validation are unsupported. Override values are stored in the workflow job and applicable email job in PostgreSQL.

Migration `0039_experimental_email_step_id.sql` enables a bounded SendGrid provider/step override subset. For subject, HTML, from, sender name, address arrays and ASCII letter/hyphen header names, the worker applies channel, legacy provider, workflow provider and matching step layers in source order. The reference-server comparison checks one conflict across layers including the source provider's additional sender and header transforms. Passthrough, other providers, integration selection, arbitrary headers and all nested merge cases are not implemented or verified. Override values remain in the PostgreSQL workflow job.

Migration `0040_experimental_email_revalidation.sql` extends email outcome states. New workflow email jobs recheck the subscriber by external ID and current email preference under a subscriber row lock before dispatch, rerender the supported template with the current profile, and cancel or fail without sending when a preference is disabled or the subscriber is absent. The reference comparison pauses the original standard queue after job creation and compares changed email/name, disabled preference, deletion, enabling a preference and adding an address through mocked SendGrid. Changes after dispatch starts, deduplicated-step mode, workflow cache behavior and actor/context deletion or concurrent changes while queued are not equivalent or unverified. Queued metadata retains templates, payload, context and override values. Each dispatch adds a subscriber/preferences query and lock, message insert and template render.

New workflow email jobs store context keys and refresh the actor profile and context data at delivery; older jobs lacking context keys retain their stored context snapshot. The original-server comparison changes the actor and context after queueing and compares the mocked SendGrid output. Actor lookup adds one query when distinct from the recipient and context lookup adds one query when keys exist. Deletion, recreation, concurrent changes and all template forms remain unverified.

New workflow email jobs recheck that their workflow exists before dispatch and compare its internal ID when configured. With the reference default step-dedup flag off, a queued email uses its stored template snapshot; a deleted or replaced workflow fails without sending. The original-server comparison covers these selected cases. Reference step-dedup hydration, cache behavior, step changes and replacement where the experimental workflow has no internal ID remain unimplemented or unverified. The workflow existence lookup is included in the existing subscriber query.

If a subscriber is deleted and recreated with the same external ID before delivery, the original and Rust compatibility mode use the new profile and address while retaining the old internal subscriber ID in the job/message history. The default Rust safety mode rejects the changed internal identity for workflow email, SMS and push; new trigger jobs also snapshot direct recipient identities. FCM prepared jobs recheck identity between targets. Set `NOVU_ALLOW_RECREATED_SUBSCRIBER=true` in API and worker processes only when the original behavior is required. Existing trigger jobs without snapshots fail in the default safety mode; standalone jobs and some topic/actor races are not fully protected or tested; already-started external requests cannot be recalled. The comparison verifies this selected scenario. Reusing an external ID for a different person can therefore deliver a previous pending notification to that person. Concurrent deletion/recreation and the full preference and message API behavior remain unverified.

## SendGrid delivery projection

Set `NOVU_SENDGRID_ENVIRONMENT_ID` and run `sendgrid-projection-worker` with database access to the queue, attempts, recipient snapshots, email messages, signed webhook events, and projection heartbeat. `NOVU_SENDGRID_PROJECTION_DB_TIMEOUT_SECONDS` defaults to 30 (1–300). The worker repeatedly scans each environment in pages of 100, locks each job, and records recipient and aggregate `sendgrid_delivery_result` values without changing the original queue status or dispatch attempt. It writes a heartbeat after each complete traversal. Locked jobs can be skipped; events arriving after a scan are reflected on a later pass. Monitor the process and heartbeat independently. Repeated full-history scans and their performance at scale are not yet validated.

Only signed events bound to the current dispatch with a verified proof, matching message and exact recipient hash are used. A permanent bounce or dropped event is failed; a temporary blocked bounce is pending; delivery and failure evidence together are conflicting. Missing subtype, duplicate addresses, unmatched current events, old sends without snapshots and other ambiguous evidence remain unknown. An accepted attempt without final evidence is pending. Aggregate results are unknown, pending, delivered, failed, partial or conflicting. A delivery event reports acceptance by the receiving server, not that a person read the message. Unverified or older dispatch events do not establish the current result; address spelling differences can prevent matching. Snapshot hashes are sensitive to dictionary guessing.

The projection is an additional database field, not an original Novu-compatible status API, and it neither changes attempt or job status nor decides retries. The integration test uses synthetic signed webhooks and a disposable Docker PostgreSQL, including reconnect and environment isolation; real SendGrid webhooks, production volume, prolonged outages and original Novu parity for this projection have not been verified.

Optional SendGrid health monitoring requires worker migration `0098`. `delivery-reconcile check-sendgrid ENV GRACE_SECONDS HEARTBEAT_MAX_SECONDS` reports aggregate overdue non-cancelled jobs and a stale or absent projector heartbeat; it exits 2 when either is unhealthy, 0 when healthy, and 1 on configuration/database errors. `check-sendgrid-metrics` emits aggregate Prometheus gauges instead and exits 0 for unhealthy measurements. For `SWALLOW_MONITOR_SCOPED_ACCESS=true`, grant the monitor role `EXECUTE` on `public.novu_rust_sendgrid_health(text,integer,integer)` and the environment-specific `alert_view` operator permission; table access is not required. Thresholds are 1–86400 seconds. The check flags every old result other than `delivered`, including unknown, failed, partial and conflicting. A heartbeat is traversal completion, not proof that every locked job was processed.

To include this optional check in `send-delivery-alert`, set both `SWALLOW_ALERT_SENDGRID_GRACE_SECONDS` and `SWALLOW_ALERT_SENDGRID_HEARTBEAT_MAX_SECONDS`; omit both to disable it. It participates in existing Slack/email alert delivery, condition masks and recovery tracking. With `SWALLOW_ALERT_CHECK_SCOPED_ACCESS=true`, the same least-privilege grant applies. Configure a scheduler and destination independently, monitor process failures and missing executions, and choose thresholds appropriate to the deployment. These checks do not send alerts without running the command or automatically resolve failed/unknown delivery. Live external alert delivery for this SendGrid condition and operation at scale have not been verified.

Worker migration `0141` adds an independent, opt-in SendGrid webhook key check to `send-delivery-alert`: set `SWALLOW_ALERT_SENDGRID_WEBHOOK_KEYS_ENABLED=true` to alert when an environment has zero or more than ten active keys. The default is false. Grant the sender DB login the environment-specific `alert_view` permission and EXECUTE on `public.novu_rust_sendgrid_webhook_key_count(text)`; direct key-table access is unnecessary, regardless of the other scoped-check setting. A failed check triggers an alert and exit 1; an unhealthy count uses the existing destination reservation and cooldown. The check reports only the count, not keys. It observes one database snapshot and cannot establish that keys are valid, match the provider, or that callbacks arrive; a missing API key table or missing permission is a check failure. Protect the function owner and schema, schedule the command, monitor failures independently, and deploy the updated API, worker migration and alert binary consistently. Docker PostgreSQL tests cover enabled/disabled states, blocked alert delivery, restricted login and environment permissions; live Slack/email delivery and provider callbacks are unverified.

A SendGrid operator `retry` resets the job and email `sendgrid_delivery_result` to `unknown` in the same transaction that removes the old attempt and records the decision. This prevents an old projection from representing the next dispatch. Apply worker migration `0097` before upgrading the reconciliation tool and retire older tools; the change does not repair retries already performed by older tools. Worker migration `0100` invalidates the old result atomically when new verified current-dispatch evidence arrives, as described below. Monitor the projector independently.

Worker migration `0099` adds `delivery-reconcile sendgrid-result ENV JOB`, a restricted view of the current SendGrid job's stored projection. It returns job status, aggregate result, attempt state and dispatch ID, current recipient kind/index/result, and the environment's last completed scan time; it does not return addresses, hashes, payloads or webhook proofs. Grant the DB login `EXECUTE ON FUNCTION public.novu_rust_sendgrid_result(text,uuid)` and the environment-specific `alert_view` operator permission. Both are required; direct table privileges are not needed. Revoke function execution separately when appropriate. Results contain operational metadata and still need access and log controls. Only current attempt recipients are listed; no snapshot is represented by an empty array and must not be read as delivery success. A result is eventually consistent, and a recent environment scan heartbeat does not prove this particular job has incorporated all events. The command does not change delivery or automatically retry and is not a Novu API. Docker PostgreSQL tests least-privilege login, environment isolation and CLI output; real provider behavior and production role deployment remain unverified.

Worker migration `0100` installs a statement-level trigger on signed SendGrid event inserts. Newly inserted, proof-verified events bound to the current dispatch and message atomically reset that job, its email messages and current recipient results to `unknown`; the projector recomputes them on its next pass. A duplicate event, invalid proof or event for an older dispatch does not cause invalidation. This is deliberately conservative and can briefly mark a delivered message unknown even for a non-terminal new event. It prevents stale success after the transaction commits but does not protect against missing callbacks, absent message links, direct table modification, or the time before an event is received. Apply API webhook migrations and worker migrations through `0099` first; the trusted migration owner needs access to the referenced tables. The trigger executes as its owner, so protect ownership and public schema changes. Upgrade all webhook instances to versions that lock job rows before inserting events; older instances do not provide the same concurrency guarantee. Each webhook batch adds queries and potentially updates all recipient snapshot rows for affected dispatches (up to 1,000 each); workload and latency at production scale are untested. Docker PostgreSQL tests signed synthetic callbacks, invalid proofs, old dispatches, duplicate events and re-projection; actual SendGrid events and sustained concurrency are unverified.

The SendGrid projector supports an optional stricter heartbeat policy. Set `NOVU_SENDGRID_PROJECTION_REQUIRE_FULL_SCAN=true` to leave the heartbeat unchanged when any listed job was skipped because it was locked or disappeared before projection. The default `false` retains the existing traversal heartbeat. With strict mode enabled on **every projector for that environment**, the existing heartbeat-age alert can detect sustained skips. Set `NOVU_SENDGRID_PROJECTION_SCAN_INTERVAL_SECONDS` to 1–300 seconds (default 30) to choose the delay between scans; scan duration is additional. Strict mode can report stale during normal contention, and a continuously locked job can keep the heartbeat stale even if other workers are making progress. Migration `0151` makes new workers reject mixed policies; older workers can still update the shared heartbeat and must be retired. Even a strict completed scan is not a consistent snapshot: newly inserted jobs or later webhooks may require another scan, and unlisted jobs without attempts are not examined. Configure thresholds above expected scan duration and interval. The Docker PostgreSQL test holds a job lock, observes a skipped scan with no heartbeat, then releases the lock and verifies recovery; production multi-worker load and failover are unverified.

Worker migration `0101` adds a least-privilege path for `delivery-reconcile list ENV [AFTER_JOB AFTER_TARGET|-]`. Set `SWALLOW_MONITOR_SCOPED_ACCESS=true` and grant the login `EXECUTE ON FUNCTION public.novu_rust_list_unknown_attempts(text,uuid,integer)` plus `alert_view` in the requested environment. This returns the same bounded job/target/status metadata as the legacy direct-table path without granting reads on queue payloads, recipient data or attempt tables. Both permissions are required; separately remove any previously granted table privileges if the role no longer needs them. Omitting the setting retains the existing direct-table behavior. Pages contain at most 100 rows and are not a consistent snapshot across concurrent changes; repeat from the beginning when monitoring. A job may have multiple FCM targets. This is a read-only view, not authorization to retry or accept an attempt, and its metadata still requires access and log controls. Docker PostgreSQL tests real login/CLI, grant revocation, other-environment denial, hidden payloads, and pagination across FCM targets. Large datasets, production database roles and arbitrary concurrent changes are unverified.

Resend and Twilio projection workers provide the same optional strict heartbeat behavior: `NOVU_RESEND_PROJECTION_REQUIRE_FULL_SCAN=true` or `NOVU_TWILIO_PROJECTION_REQUIRE_FULL_SCAN=true` suppresses that environment's heartbeat when a listed job is skipped. The default is `false`. Their scan intervals are independently selectable with `NOVU_RESEND_PROJECTION_SCAN_INTERVAL_SECONDS` and `NOVU_TWILIO_PROJECTION_SCAN_INTERVAL_SECONDS` (1–300 seconds, default 30, excluding scan duration). Use strict mode on every corresponding worker before relying on the heartbeat alert. Migration `0151` rejects mixed policies among new workers; older workers can still update the shared row. Contention can cause stale-heartbeat alerts even when other workers are progressing; choose thresholds and policy for the deployment. A full traversal is not an atomic snapshot and does not cover new events/jobs after traversal or jobs lacking attempts. Docker PostgreSQL tests both actual workers with locked rows, skip observation, heartbeat suppression, recovery and invalid settings; sustained production concurrency and real provider failure are unverified.

Worker migration `0102` installs an owner-executed trigger on Resend event inserts. A newly inserted authenticated event with an email ID matching a current attempt in the same environment atomically resets the matching job and email projections to `unknown`; later scans recompute them. The trigger always locks matching jobs, even if already unknown, so a concurrent projector cannot commit an older success after the event without the invalidation following it. Duplicate event IDs (the API's conflict-update path) and unrelated IDs do not invalidate. If multiple current attempts share a provider ID, all are invalidated conservatively. The trigger adds indexed lookups and a job update for each matched job, including already-unknown jobs, and contention or deadlock may make the webhook request fail so that its provider must retry. Apply API migration `0010` before worker `0102`, and protect the trigger function owner and public schema.
